# Decision Register

Log every decision worth remembering here: **date**, **status**, **reason**.

Before proposing or researching an approach, check this register first — don't
re-suggest something already tried and rejected without saying so. It's fine to
resurface a rejected approach if the prior rejection may have been wrong or
there's no viable alternative, but say explicitly that it was tried before and
why it's worth revisiting.

| Date | Decision | Status | Reason |
|------|----------|--------|--------|
| 2026-08-25 | Adopted [`claude-code-managed-repo-template`](https://github.com/QuickWaller/claude-code-managed-repo-template) (MIT) conventions: `Working.md`, `memory/`, `decisions/`, the Rules section, `.claude/agents` (`executor`, `researcher`) | accepted | Same pattern already in use across sibling projects; credited in `CLAUDE.md`. `handoffs/` and `integrations/` deliberately not adopted — both are opt-in and this repo has no parallel work streams yet. |
| 2026-08-25 | Named the project **df-overseer** (folder on disk still `df-automation`) | accepted | "Overseer" is the DF community's own term for the person running a fortress — literally the role the agent takes. The `df-` prefix keeps it discoverable for a public repo. `df-automation` undersold it once automation became the smallest of three layers. |
| 2026-08-25 | **The world persists; fortresses are mortal.** When a fort dies the overseer reclaims or re-embarks in the same world; the chronicle carries forward | accepted | Matches how the community actually accumulates long histories (succession forts). Makes a fort loss into content rather than an outage. Turned out to be load-bearing twice more: fort death is the *training signal* for cross-fort learning, and re-embarking in one world lets you control for world variance when comparing designs. |
| 2026-08-25 | **The model is never shown a rendered map** — not ASCII, not a tile grid, not a screenshot. All spatial facts are computed in code and asserted in text | accepted | Documented LLM spatial blindness: transformers read a 2D grid as a 1D token sequence and cannot reliably reconstruct adjacency. Independently corroborated by roguelike-accessibility research and NetHack agent work. See `research/2026-08-25-spatial-perception.md`. |
| 2026-08-25 | Named landmarks + relative direction/distance are the primary model-facing representation; coordinates demoted to a code-only field | accepted | Changed by cross-domain prior art: MUD room-and-exits graphs (an idiom LLMs have deep training priors for) and blind-gamer accessibility research, which asks for "5 East 3 North" over absolute position. |
| 2026-08-25 | **The agent brain lives outside this repo.** This repo exposes DF over MCP; the driving agent is a config change | accepted | Revised mid-session from the opposite position. Both candidate hosts are MCP clients, so the boundary makes the brain swappable, keeps this repo useful to anyone regardless of model, and keeps model/infra specifics out of a public repo. |
| 2026-08-25 | Deferred the choice between `openclaw` and `hermes-agent` as the brain | proposed | Both have built-in cron, MCP clients, and persistent memory; neither is disqualified. An initial claim that hermes' scheduler was the differentiator was wrong — openclaw's is better (heartbeat, self-pacing `/loop`, exponential retry backoff). The loop shape isn't known yet, and the MCP boundary makes it a swap. Tiebreaker will be empirical: which survives 30 days unattended. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Learning is outcome tracking, not self-critique.** Decisions record a prediction and a check date; the game state is the judge | accepted | LLM self-assessment is confident and largely uncorrelated with truth. This domain supplies rare ground truth — the fortress either recovers or it doesn't. Failed predictions are the objective learning signal. |
| 2026-08-25 | Lessons carry a **scope tag** (universal / world / site) and enter as **hypotheses with an evidence counter**, promoting to doctrine only on repetition | **superseded** (same day, by the row below) | The problem framing held; the mechanism did not. See `research/2026-08-25-learning-architecture.md`. |
| 2026-08-25 | **The DF wiki is a source of hypotheses, not doctrine** — wiki claims enter the same evidence pipeline with a higher prior | accepted | Keeps received knowledge (what the community believes) distinct from verified knowledge (what this overseer has confirmed). Bootstraps past the ~20-fort sample-size limit without bypassing the learning loop, and lets the agent disagree with the wiki on its own evidence — a better read than a walkthrough. Wiki is MIT + GFDL dual-licensed (*verified*); bulk download permitted with attribution. |
| 2026-08-25 | Build the **perception eval harness first** — hand-written briefings, questions with known answers | accepted | Perception is the project's biggest risk and everything else is downstream of it. Needs no running game and no agent, so it tests the risky thing in an afternoon rather than after building an agent on top of it. |
| 2026-08-25 | Streaming to the site: **periodic screenshot + live dashboard**, not 24/7 video | proposed | At `FPS_CAP:5` almost nothing changes in 30s, so a still conveys the fortress about as well as video at a fraction of the CPU/bandwidth — and the hypervisor is already running DF. The distinctive content is the chronicle and the overseer's reasoning beside the image, not the image. One dashboard serves the wall display, phone, willsmith.nz, and a shareable artifact. |
| 2026-08-25 | Public repo from day one; infra specifics in gitignored `infra/local.*` with committed `.example` counterparts | accepted | User requirement. Forces clean docs and no hardcoded paths, while keeping Proxmox/Coolify/IP details out of a public repo. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Convention: research cross-domain prior art before designing from scratch** | accepted | User correction mid-session: "i cant be the first to come across such a problem." Restating the problem domain-neutrally and reading other fields (MUDs, accessibility, robotics scene graphs, RTS terrain analysis, space syntax) changed three concrete design decisions that DF-only research had settled wrongly. Subagents default to staying inside the domain given — the instruction must be explicit in the brief. |
| 2026-08-25 | Rejected: headless/terminal DF | rejected | `PRINT_MODE:TEXT` has not existed since v50 in either the Steam or Classic build; "Classic mode" is ASCII-styled graphics, still SDL, still mouse-oriented. Older writeups describing ncurses-in-a-PTY are v0.47-era. Non-Steam buys nothing here. |
| 2026-08-25 | Rejected: DFPlex for multiplayer | rejected | Dead for v50+. It worked by rapidly entering/exiting menus and shipping a screenshot per player, tightly bound to pre-Steam render code; maintainers' own verdict is that it needs rebuilding from scratch. Irrelevant anyway — agents need structured state, not rendered views, so two agents on one fort works today over shared RPC. |
| 2026-08-25 | Rejected: adventure mode concurrent with an active fortress | rejected | Playing any mode *locks* the world (*verified*); "Start new game from existing world" only appears with no active fort or adventurer. The "Save to a new Timeline" fork allows concurrency but the worlds diverge permanently. Time-sliced visiting (agent retires → human adventures → agent unretires) works and needs no scripting, since a person drives the UI. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Replaced the evidence counter.** Hypotheses are weighted by *diagnosticity* (Van Evera hoop / smoking-gun tests), stratified by recorded covariates, with promotion thresholds derived from a stated error rate (Wald SPRT) rather than an arbitrary count. Attribution happens at the **feature** level, never the decision level | accepted | The counter silently assumed credit assignment was already solved: you cannot record that a fort "supports" a hypothesis without already knowing which of ~200 decisions caused its death. It presupposed the very problem it was meant to help with. Sharp consequence: **the fort ledger's schema defines what is learnable** — an unrecorded feature can never become a lesson. |
| 2026-08-25 | Scope tags keep the taxonomy but drop the three discrete buckets — use a **hierarchical model with partial pooling** | accepted | A lesson is rarely purely universal or purely world-local; how general it is should be estimated from evidence, not fixed by a judgement call made at write time. |
| 2026-08-25 | Wiki claims enter as a **stated prior strength** (community consensus, source agreement, version namespace), not a magic starting count | accepted | Corrects the same arbitrary-arithmetic mistake as the promotion counter. The instinct — received knowledge as prior, not doctrine — was right. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Deliberate experimentation promoted to v1**, from "probably not v1" | accepted | Passive learning at N≈20 is close to unworkable — twenty forts in twenty worlds are twenty confounded anecdotes. One-variable-at-a-time is nearly free once the ledger exists, and re-embarking in the same world holds the largest confound constant. Another payoff from the world-persists decision. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Doctrine size is a hard, measured budget, not a style preference** | accepted | A 2026 controlled study (arXiv:2607.19257, five models incl. Claude Sonnet 5, four formats, both placements, 960 calls/model) found perfect-response rate collapses to zero by **N=80 simultaneous rules**. *Single study, threshold unreplicated* — treat as order-of-magnitude and measure our own compliance-vs-doctrine-size curve early. |
| 2026-08-25 | **The overseer is not a long-running conversation.** Each turn assembles a fresh context from memory; continuity lives in the memory stores, not in message history | accepted | Microsoft/Salesforce (arXiv:2505.06120) measured ~39% accuracy loss as conversations extend. A month-long fortress session naively implemented is one enormous multi-turn conversation. Also rules out the "resident session" loop shape floated earlier. Pairs with "lost in the middle": doctrine goes at the front of context (possibly duplicated at the end), never a middle tier — making the cache-ordering rule a *correctness* requirement, not just cost. |
| 2026-08-25 | **All learning grading is mechanical**, never the agent's self-report; plus an automated auditor cross-checking cited doctrine against actual actions | accepted | A model can produce a compelling account of why doctrine changed its decision without that doctrine having influenced anything (unfaithful-CoT research). Removing the model from the grading loop is the only defence with evidence behind it. Watch-for heuristic: doctrine that only ever grows and is never walked back across 20 forts signals confirmation, not learning. |
| 2026-08-25 | Fort ledger moved to **earliest build item** | accepted | It is a trial registry — nothing else in the learning design is checkable without it, and its schema determines what can ever be learned. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Build the memory layer ourselves, framework-agnostic**, exposed as MCP tools. Memory lives **in this repo** as git-tracked files | accepted | Settles the open question of where memory physically lives. Consistent with "the brain lives outside this repo" — any host can drive it. Keeps the most interesting artifact public, diffable and reviewable, and preserves the no-self-editing divergence below, which adopting Letta would fight. Cost: we build paging/retrieval ourselves rather than inheriting it. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Deliberate divergence from Letta/MemGPT: the model does not edit its own memory.** Promotion is mechanical, grading is code-driven | accepted | Letta's defining feature is LLM-managed memory via tool calls; we do the opposite. Self-managed memory is where unfaithfulness and sycophancy live — the field considers it live enough to have a benchmark for *sycophancy in agent memory*. For a system whose output is accumulated claims about what works, an agent that can quietly rewrite its own evidence is the precise failure mode to design against. See `memory/agent-memory-standards.md`. |
| 2026-08-25 | **Compaction inside a turn; memory stores across turns** — corrects the over-absolute "fresh context per turn" | accepted | Anthropic's long-running-agent stack pairs compaction *with* memory rather than choosing. A single overseer turn involves many tool calls (briefing → landmark lookups → site ranking) and that inner loop grows. Their reported 100-turn result — 84% token savings **and** 39% performance improvement — indicates context bloat is a correctness problem, not just a cost one. |
| 2026-08-25 | Proxmox access: dedicated narrow user, **privilege separation off**, everything scoped to the `df-overseer` resource pool | accepted | Privsep only earns its doubled ACL work when the *user* is broad and the *token* should be narrower; with a narrow user it buys nothing and halves the setup. Pool scoping is the real boundary — the token cannot see or touch anything outside it. |
| 2026-08-25 | **`VM.Allocate` granted** (create/destroy), pool-scoped — reversing the initial recommendation to exclude it | accepted | User's call, and the case against was weaker than first framed: granted on `/pool/df-overseer` it can only create/destroy within that pool, and the valuable artifacts (chronicle, doctrine, ledger, memory) live in this repo rather than the VM. Residual risk is losing the DF VM itself. The remaining argument against — that the overseer's remit already includes "abandon and restart," making "destroy this VM" an adjacent category error — is noted in `docs/PROXMOX-SETUP.md` and should inform how lifecycle actions are gated in code. |
| 2026-08-25 | Proxmox is at `<pve-host>:8006` (PVE 9.x), reached via the tailnet `<tailnet-a>` subnet router for `<lan-subnet>/24` | accepted | Not `<redacted-ip>` as first assumed — that host does not exist. `ping` reported 0% loss because `<redacted-ip>` was answering with ICMP *Destination host unreachable*, which Windows counts as a reply. Also: `VM.Monitor` was removed in PVE 9, so privilege lists written against 8.x are wrong. |
| 2026-08-26 | **Drop Steam on the VM; use DF Classic (free, Bay12) there.** Keep playing the Steam version locally | accepted | Removes the biggest month-long-run hazard: Steam auto-updating DF mid-fort shifts memory offsets and silently breaks DFHack. Also removes Steam Guard 2FA from the provisioning script, the Steam client's RAM cost, and the "force Steam Linux Runtime 1.0 (scout) for both DF and DFHack" gotcha. Cost is the premium tileset — softened because the agent never sees graphics (commitment #1), Classic supports custom tilesets, and Stonesense works regardless. Same engine version, so saves should be interchangeable (**verify, don't assume**). |
| 2026-08-26 | Second role `DFOverseerNode` (`Sys.Audit` + `Sys.AccessNetwork`) bound at `/nodes/<pve-node>`, propagate ON | accepted | **`Sys.*` privileges are only checked at `/nodes/<node>` or `/` — never against a pool or storage path.** Adding `Sys.AccessNetwork` to `DFOverseer` did nothing because that role is bound only at `/pool/df-overseer` and `/storage/<storage>`. Cost three failed diagnoses (`Datastore.AllocateTemplate`, then `Sys.AccessNetwork`-in-the-role, then a suspected token-vs-password limitation) before reading `/access/permissions` directly showed no node binding existed. A separate narrow role keeps node scope to read-status + fetch-URL rather than binding all 24 privileges there. |
| 2026-08-26 | `VM.Clone` and the five `VM.GuestAgent.*` privileges granted, pool-scoped | accepted | `VM.Clone` is required to clone our *own* template and was confirmed missing by test — it does not reach templates outside the pool. `VM.GuestAgent.Unrestricted` permits arbitrary command execution inside pool VMs, which allows guest provisioning without SSH or guest networking; granted knowingly, and recorded here because it is a large capability that should not be used silently. |
| 2026-08-26 | **Dedicated SSH keypair `~/.ssh/df_overseer_ed25519`** (ed25519, no passphrase) for the DF VM, rather than reusing `claude_vm` or `claude_dev_vm_ed25519` | accepted | A shared key makes revocation indivisible — rotating it for this project would lock out unrelated VMs. No passphrase is deliberate and is the cost of unattended month-long operation; the containment is that the key reaches the DF VM only and is not a Proxmox credential. Key material stays in `~/.ssh`; `.env` holds paths (`DF_SSH_KEY`, `DF_SSH_PUBKEY`). Windows note: `chmod 600` does nothing on NTFS, `icacls /inheritance:r` is what restricts it. |
| 2026-08-26 | DF VM sized **6144 MB max / 2048 MB balloon, 4 cores** | accepted | User's call, taken with 5.5 GB free on a 15.5 GB host. KVM backs only the pages the guest touches, so idle DF sits far below the cap and the balloon floor gives the host something to reclaim; the worldgen spike is the real peak. Memory is a one-line change on a stopped VM, so this is revisable. The standing rule that came with it: **read `/nodes/<node>/status` before starting the VM** — free memory on this host moved 13.7 → 4.8 → 9.0 GB used inside two days and the other VMs are invisible to us. |
| 2026-08-26 | Ask the human to add the **`import` content type** to `<storage>` rather than granting the role `Datastore.Allocate` | accepted | `import-from` refuses an `iso`-class volume ("needs to be 'images' or 'import'"), so the cloud image downloaded as an ISO is unusable as a disk source. `download-url` does accept `content=import`, so one tick of a checkbox makes the entire template build scriptable with no host shell. The alternative — granting `Datastore.Allocate` at `/storage/<storage>` — would let the token edit and delete storage definitions, which is a large standing capability bought for a single one-time flag. Confirmed by denial that the token cannot do it itself: `GET /storage/<storage>` → `403 (Datastore.Allocate)`. |
| 2026-08-26 | Perception eval fixtures are **machine-readable fortresses**, not hand-written briefings; every expected answer is computed from the fixture | accepted | The build-order item said "hand-written briefings, questions with known answers". Hand-writing both the briefing and the answer means writing the same fact twice, and a fixture edit silently leaves stale answers behind. Deriving both from one fixture also makes the representation a *variable*: the same fortress renders three ways and the comparison is fair by construction. |
| 2026-08-26 | Representations declare what they `provide`; questions declare what they `need`; the runner **skips** combinations rather than scoring them wrong | accepted | Scoring a representation on a fact its encoding never carried measures nothing. The skip list is itself the most useful output: it names exactly which spatial facts the agent cannot derive from a briefing, and therefore which zoom tools it must be given. `exits_v1`/`prose_v1` skip `bearing_far` and `zlevel_far`. |
| 2026-08-26 | **No ASCII-map control arm in the perception eval** | rejected | The obvious fourth arm, deliberately not built: it would require writing the rendered-map generator the project committed never to write (commitment #1), and the comparison it would produce is already settled by the LessWrong and BALROG evidence the commitment rests on. Revisit only if the commitment itself is re-opened. |
| 2026-08-27 | Perception eval results (`evals/perception/results/*.jsonl`) are **tracked in git**, not gitignored | accepted | A scoped `evals/perception/.gitignore` was silently excluding every run's output. Reversed because the project now has a stated goal — a public-facing report built from accumulated experiment data — that a gitignored results directory would quietly defeat. Raw JSONL is small and diffable; keep it. |
| 2026-08-27 | Fixed: `response_schema()`'s `confidence` field carried `minimum`/`maximum` on a `number` type | accepted | Found on the harness's first-ever live API call (all 20/20 smoke-test cells failed with the same 400) — a case the stub-only tests couldn't catch, exactly the gap the README flagged. Anthropic's structured-output schema validator rejects `minimum`/`maximum` on `number`. Fix: drop the constraint keywords, state the 0-1 range in the schema `description` instead (a prompt-level constraint, not a schema one). Re-run after the fix: 20/20 succeeded, 100% accuracy on `coords_v1`, cache reads confirmed non-zero (12/20 requests, 26,738 tokens) — the prefix-caching design works as intended. |
| 2026-08-27 | **First real signal on the core project bet: `exits_v1` ties `coords_v1`** | proposed | 120-cell stratified run (`--per-category 1`, all 3 fixtures, all 3 representations; `evals/perception/results/stratified-2026-08-27.jsonl`), actual cost $0.27. All three representations scored 97.4% on the shared question set — the landmark-and-exits idiom lost nothing to being handed raw coordinates. Soft spot: `route` (multi-step pathing) at 66.7%, identically across all three representations (n=3), reading as the model finding this graph hard rather than a representation weakness. Notable but thin: confidence separation (right vs. wrong) was +0.04 for `coords_v1` (can't tell when it's wrong) vs. +0.25 for `exits_v1` — if it replicates, an argument for the landmark idiom beyond parity. **Status `proposed` not `accepted`**: n=1-3 per category on 15-landmark hand-authored fixtures, not the real lossier production generator — a first signal, not a conclusion. The full 342-cell matrix (3x repeats) would tighten `route` and calibration specifically, est. $0.50-1 more given now-known real per-cell cost. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Core bet confirmed at full sample: `exits_v1` ties `coords_v1`, n=108/representation** | accepted | Full 342-cell matrix run (`evals/perception/results/full-2026-08-27.jsonl`), actual cost $0.83, zero API errors. All three representations: 99.1% on the shared question set. `route` rose to 88.9% (8/9) with more data, still identical across representations — reads as model/graph difficulty, not a representation weakness. **Promoted from `proposed` to `accepted`**: this is n=108/representation on the hand-authored fixtures, enough to call the core bet supported *for this test's scope* (see standing caveats below — still not the real lossier production generator, still only 15-landmark fixtures). |
| 2026-08-27 | **Retracted: the calibration-separation finding from the 120-cell run.** It did not replicate | accepted | At n=108, calibration separation reordered: `prose_v1` best (+0.25), `exits_v1` middling (+0.09), `coords_v1` still worst (+0.04) — versus the 120-cell run's `exits_v1` best (+0.25), `coords_v1` worst (+0.04). The specific representation ranking was noise at n=1-3/category, exactly as the smaller run's own caveat warned. What *did* hold across both runs: `coords_v1` has consistently the weakest right/wrong confidence separation — raw coordinates don't help the model know when it's wrong, either. Recorded as a correction rather than quietly overwriting the earlier entry, per the standing "verify the verification" rule. |
| 2026-08-27 | New consideration on the still-open `openclaw`/`hermes-agent` decision (row above, 2026-08-25): **multi-agent decomposition by spatial task** | proposed | User's observation: today's eval tested comprehension (bearing/distance/adjacency over *existing* landmarks), not judgment (where to route a patrol, where to place a new room) — a gap the harness's own README already names ("does not test judgment, only comprehension"). Judgment-level placement in open space is a harder place for spatial confusion to hide than factual recall. Splitting patrol/military, construction/placement, and economy into separate specialized agents — rather than one generalist — narrows each agent's spatial-reasoning surface, which plays directly to that weakness. Relevant to the brain choice because openclaw's multi-agent support is a candidate differentiator alongside its scheduler. Does not resolve the deferred choice; adds a criterion to evaluate it against. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Fort ledger built: JSONL, git-tracked, one row per fort** (`ledger/`) | accepted | Earliest build item per the 2026-08-25 entry, now done as schema + write path + stratification, no inference. JSONL over SQLite because at N~20 a full scan is instant, rows diff individually in `git log`, and a file readable with `cat` serves the public-report goal that a binary store would not. If queries outgrow it, SQLite becomes a *derived read model* rebuilt from the JSONL, never the system of record. Same reasoning that tracked the perception eval results. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Design features are split into orthogonal axes**, replacing the design doc's single `entrance_design` field | accepted | `docs/MEMORY-ARCHITECTURE.md`'s sketch bundled several independent choices into one label. Build item 8 (one-variable-at-a-time experiment selector) is meaningless against a portmanteau field: two forts recorded as `entrance_design: "sealed trap corridor"` vs something else differ in three ways at once and cannot be compared. Split into `entrance_count` / `entrance_seal` / `entrance_traps`, and similarly elsewhere. At N~20 a bundled field silently destroys the only trials available. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Every ledger field declares a `source`** (`MECHANICAL` / `DERIVED` / `HUMAN` / `AGENT`), and `store.assert_gradeable()` refuses to let grading code read the last two | accepted | Not in the research spec; added because "never grade the agent's account of its own learning" is easy to state and easy to erode one convenient field at a time. Enforcing it in the store means future evidence-update code that reaches for `notes` or a human-written `rationale` fails loudly instead of quietly folding narration into evidence. Corollary: `observations[]` are HUMAN-sourced and therefore not gradeable, so they carry `supporting_fields` (dotted paths into the mechanical record) to stay checkable. |
| 2026-08-27 | **`unrecorded` is a sentinel that stratification drops rather than pools**, and the dropped count is part of the result | accepted | Section 3.2's warning is that an unrecorded feature can never become a lesson. A caller free to treat "we did not look" as a value would defeat the schema's purpose, so `store.stratify` does not offer that option and reports the honest denominator: two strata of 3 that discarded 9 rows is a different claim from one that discarded none. |
| 2026-08-27 | **The ledger can record our own failures**: `agent_error`, `human_intervention`, `fps_collapse`, `crash_or_corruption`, and status `run_ended_technical` | accepted | The stated goal is a public report built from accumulated data. A schema with nowhere to write "the overseer walled its own dwarves in" or "we stopped because FPS collapsed" produces a flattering, wrong account by construction. `docs/MEMORY-ARCHITECTURE.md`'s own heuristic (a learning system should occasionally be embarrassed by its past doctrine) is unenforceable if the embarrassment has no field. The selftest asserts these vocabulary members exist. |
| 2026-08-27 | Ledger schema is **proposed, not verified**: no `MECHANICAL` field has been confirmed readable from DFHack (`schema.MECHANICAL_PATH_VERIFIED = False`) | proposed | There is no perception layer yet to confirm against, so every `MECHANICAL` claim is a bet that code will be able to read that value from game state. `defense_depth`, `primary_industry` and `surface_footprint` are the likeliest to have no clean mechanical reading; if so they get demoted to `AGENT` and become colour rather than evidence. Discovering this now is far cheaper than after twenty forts, which is the whole reason the schema was built before the game side. |
| 2026-08-27 | **DF VM dropped to 4096 MB / 2048 MB balloon and started**, revising the 2026-08-26 sizing of 6144 MB | accepted | User's call, taken because a cluster upgrade with more memory per host is planned, so sizing for today's single 15.5 GiB host is not worth waiting on. 4096 cleared the host-memory gate with 1.3 GiB headroom where 6144 did not. First boot ever: cloud-init completed, disk resized, SSH key worked, guest reports 3915 MB. Memory is a one-line change on a stopped VM, so this stays revisable upward once the cluster lands. |
| 2026-08-27 | Added `set-memory` and `start` subcommands to `scripts/provision_vm.py`, with `start` gated on host `available` memory | accepted | The 2026-08-26 standing rule ("read `/nodes/<node>/status` before starting the VM") was a rule a human had to remember; now the code enforces it and refuses to start when under 1 GiB would remain, `--force` to override. `set-memory` refuses to run against a running VM, because a live `memory` write goes through the balloon driver and does not move the ceiling, so it would report a success that did not happen. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Template gap found: `agent: enabled=1` without installing `qemu-guest-agent` in the guest** | accepted | Proxmox-side enablement only opens the virtio channel. Without the package inside the guest every `/agent/*` call returns 500 and the API cannot report the VM's IP, which is how VM 104 had to be located by port-scanning `<lan-subnet>/24` instead. Installed by hand on 104 and verified the API can now read `<df-vm-ip>`. **`cmd_build_template` still has the gap** and will reproduce it in the next template. |
| 2026-08-27 | **NIC MACs are derived from the vmid and pinned at clone time** (`mac_for_vmid`, `MAC_OVERRIDES`), so a DHCP reservation survives a rebuild | accepted | User reserves addresses on the router by convention, and asked the right question: what happens when the VM is deleted and recreated. Proxmox generates a random MAC every time a NIC is created, *including on clone*, so a rebuild would orphan the reservation and the symptom would read as "the static IP stopped working" rather than "the MAC changed". Pinning is done in `cmd_clone`, not `cmd_build_template`, because clone regenerates whatever the template had. VM 104's existing `<reserved-mac>` is kept as an override rather than renumbered, so the reservation live today keeps working; the derived scheme (`BC:24:11:00:hi:lo`, Proxmox's own OUI) applies to new VMs. Side effect worth having: with the address known before boot, the guest agent is no longer the only way to find a VM. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Guest packages are baked by booting the template once and installing over SSH**, rejecting the `cicustom` cloud-init route | accepted | Closes the 2026-08-27 template gap above. Proxmox's native cloud-init fields cannot install a package; that needs `cicustom` pointing at a **snippets** volume, and `<storage>` carries `import,images,iso`. Enabling snippets needs `Datastore.Allocate` at `/storage` (the token has only `AllocateSpace`/`AllocateTemplate`) and the snippet file itself has no API upload path, so it would need placing on the host filesystem. That is two human steps before any automation starts, and the user's constraint is that this runs unattended. Same reasoning as the 2026-08-26 `import` entry, reaching the opposite conclusion because that one bought a whole scriptable build for one checkbox, where this one buys a single package for a permission grant plus host shell access. **Correction:** `Working.md` recorded this fix as "a two-line cloud-init change" and it is not; that claim was never checked. |
| 2026-08-27 | The template bake takes a **static build address from `DF_BUILD_IP`**, released before conversion | accepted | The bake must SSH into the VM before the guest agent exists, and the agent is exactly what would otherwise report the VM's address, so the build is circular without a known address. One gitignored `.env` value breaks it, in the same category as `DF_SSH_KEY` and the other host specifics. `DF_BUILD_GW` defaults to `.1` of the same `/24`. `ipconfig0` is reset to `ip=dhcp` in a `finally`, on the failure path too, so no clone can ever inherit the build address. |
| 2026-08-27 | The bake **verifies `/agent/ping` and `/agent/network-get-interfaces` while the VM is still running**, and seals cloud-init state before conversion | accepted | Standing "verify the verification" rule: a template that silently lacks a working agent is the precise failure this change exists to prevent, and once converted the next chance to notice is a clone. The bake fails if the agent does not report the build address back. Sealing (`cloud-init clean --logs --seed`, remove `/etc/ssh/ssh_host_*`, truncate `/etc/machine-id`) is the other half: a template cloned from a booted VM otherwise hands every clone that VM's instance-id, machine-id and SSH host keys. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Template 101 rebuilt at 4096 MB with the guest agent baked in**, and the bake verified end to end | accepted | First actual run of `bake_template`: ssh up in 17s, cloud-init completed, `qemu-guest-agent` installed, `POST /agent/ping` returned `{'result': {}}` and the agent reported `<build-ip>` over the virtio channel. Built at 4096 rather than the old 6144 so clones inherit the size VM 104 actually runs at, retiring the housekeeping item about clones needing a resize. `net0` came out `BC:24:11:00:00:65` (0x65 = 101), confirming clone-time MAC pinning runs. **Not yet verified: nothing has been cloned from it**, so sealing (fresh machine-id and host keys per clone) is still unproven. |
| 2026-08-27 | `resize_disk()` **retries the post-import resize on timeout** | accepted | The first build died on `qemu-img resize '--preallocation=metadata' ... failed: got timeout`, firing immediately after a 25 GB import while the storage was still flushing. A manual retry completed in **3 seconds**, so it is load, not failure. Retrying beats discarding a build that has already copied 25 GB. Retries only on timeout and re-raises anything else; growing a disk is idempotent, so a retry after a partial success cannot shrink it. |
| 2026-08-27 | Agent **command** endpoints are POST, **info** endpoints are GET | accepted | `GET /agent/ping` returns `501 Method not implemented`, which reads exactly like a missing agent. The verification written to catch a broken agent would therefore have failed against a working one. `ping`, `shutdown` and `fsfreeze-*` are POST; `network-get-interfaces`, `get-osinfo` and friends answer GET. Recorded because the error message actively misleads. |
| 2026-08-27 | The bake **hard-stops the VM from the hypervisor** after sealing, rather than asking the guest to power itself off | accepted | Sealing truncates `/etc/machine-id` and removes `/var/lib/dbus/machine-id`, which is what systemd needs to reach dbus, so a `systemctl poweroff` issued after it never completes: the VM ran until the 300s wait expired. Sealing must happen while the VM is up, so the guest cannot be the thing that shuts it down. The seal command now ends in `sync`, which is what makes a hard stop safe. Compounding cause worth noting: `check=False` on that ssh call swallowed the error, so the only symptom was a timeout 300 seconds later. |
| 2026-08-27 | **A failed build tears down the VM it created**, `--keep-failed` to override | accepted | User's call. A half-built VM is neither a usable template nor obviously junk, and the next run picks a different vmid rather than noticing it. Tension acknowledged and deliberately accepted: both bugs above were diagnosed *because* the broken VM was still there, so the teardown discards exactly the evidence that made today's debugging cheap. `--keep-failed` is the escape hatch, and richer failure capture (task logs, guest console) is the thing to add if the teardown starts costing more than the clutter it prevents. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Sealing verified end to end by cloning template 101** (VM 105 `df-seal-test`, linked) | accepted | The gap the previous entry flagged, now closed. The clone's SSH host key (`...ICasVMj1Suqd8...`) differs from the build VM's (`...IDZ3bZeQuIpIX...`, recovered from the stray `nul` file), with an mtime from this boot; `machine-id` is repopulated, `instance-id` is fresh with a single instance dir, hostname took the clone's name, cloud-init reports `done`, `qemu-guest-agent` is `active`. **The payoff: the API reported the clone's address (`<clone-ip>`) with no static configuration anywhere** — the capability whose absence forced VM 104 to be located by port-scanning `<lan-subnet>/24`. `cmd_clone`'s MAC pinning also ran for the first time, producing `BC:24:11:00:00:69` (0x69 = 105). |
| 2026-08-27 | **`next_vmid()` is the only safe source of a vmid**, never a guess | accepted | Cloning to a hand-picked `--vmid 102` failed with `500 unable to create VM 102: config file already exists`. VM 102 exists on this host *outside* the `df-overseer` pool, so it is invisible to our token — `pool_members()` cannot see it and `GET /vms/102` returns `403 VM.Audit`. The pool view is not the host view, and any id chosen by eye can collide with a VM we are structurally unable to enumerate. |
| 2026-08-27 | **DF Classic + DFHack installed and proven on VM 104**, retiring the untested 2026-08-26 decision to drop Steam | accepted | DF v0.53.16 linux64 (build tag `ITCH`) from Bay 12 plus DFHack **53.16-r1.1**, the exact version of the Windows install, so `memory/dfhack-environment.md`'s findings carry over. Both tarballs are flat and DFHack extracts over the game root, with no `dfhooks` split like the Windows Steam layout. Proven, not merely installed: DF runs headless, `dfhack-run lua` executes remotely, and the RPC server listens on `127.0.0.1:5000` owned by the `dwarfort` process. Details in `infra/local.df-vm-install.md`. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Headless DF via Xvfb**, revisiting the 2026-08-25 rejection of headless DF | accepted | That rejection stands on its own terms: `PRINT_MODE:TEXT` still does not exist and there is still no text mode. What it did not consider is that none of that matters when nobody looks at the window. A virtual framebuffer (`Xvfb :99 -screen 0 1280x800x24`) costs a few MB, needs no GPU, and satisfies SDL, which was the actual blocker. Design commitment #1 is untouched: the framebuffer exists so DF will start, and the model never sees it. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Saves live at `~/.local/share/Bay 12 Games/Dwarf Fortress/save/`**, not in the game directory | accepted | Recorded because it is a silent-failure trap, not a preference. The Linux build uses the XDG data path, while most community writeups and every pre-v50 habit point at `<df>/data/save`. A backup or snapshot script written against the old layout finds no such directory and reports success having copied nothing, which is precisely the failure mode that matters for a fortress meant to run a month. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Worlds are generated from the command line** (`./dfhack -gen <id> <seed> "<preset>"`), default preset **`POCKET ISLAND`** | accepted | Removes UI driving from the one part of setup that would otherwise need it: `-gen` runs silently and quits. Preset names are compiled into the binary, not in any config file. `POCKET ISLAND` is 17x17 and stops at year 30, producing a ~900 KB save in ~12 seconds; the user's call on 2026-08-27 was tiny worlds for now, and it matches `docs/PURPOSE.md`'s small-world, short-history discipline. |
| 2026-08-27 | **`-gen` failures must be detected by the absence of the region directory, not by exit code** | accepted | Two of eight runs exited 1 after generating the full history into `save/current`, never renaming it and never writing an export, with no message in `gamelog.txt`, `errorlog.txt`, stdout or stderr. Roughly a quarter of runs, and completely silent. Not caused by DFHack: it reproduces with DFHack's hooks loaded either way. Retrying with a fresh seed works. Exit 134 (SIGABRT) is a different and documented case, the world id already existing. |
| 2026-08-27 | **Worldgen is not the memory spike it was assumed to be**; 4096 MB stands, swap added anyway | accepted | Sampled at 0.5s across a whole `POCKET ISLAND` gen: peak RSS **561 MB**, swap untouched, available memory never below 2.5 GiB. The standing worry that worldgen would blow through 4096 MB with no swap was unfounded at this world size. The 4 GB swapfile (`vm.swappiness=10`, in `/etc/fstab`) was added first and has never been touched, so it is now insurance rather than a fix. **Scope: this measures worldgen only.** A long-running fort with hundreds of units is the real memory question and is still unmeasured. |
| 2026-08-27 | **`init.txt` settings are matched by token name, never by line number** | accepted | Found while reconciling the docs against the VM: `docs/PURPOSE.md` and `memory/dfhack-environment.md` both cited `FPS_CAP` and `G_FPS_CAP` as `prefs/init.txt` lines 22 and 23, which is true of the local Windows install and wrong for the Linux one, where they are lines 71 and 75. A line-seeking script would not fail, it would edit whatever else lives there. Both docs corrected to name the tokens and to say explicitly that the numbers differ per install. |
| 2026-08-28 | **Infra records move to gitignored `infra/local.*`; committed files carry placeholders** | accepted | User's call, prompted by a pre-push scan. `memory/proxmox-access.md` had been breaking CLAUDE.md's own public-repo rule since it was written, and today's `df-vm-install.md` joined it: both are made largely of addresses, hostnames, MACs and pool scopes. Both are now untracked. Tracked files use `<pve-host>`, `<df-vm-ip>`, `<lan-subnet>`, `<tailnet-a>`, `<reserved-mac>`, and `memory/MEMORY.md` gained a "local only" section so a cloner knows the files exist and why they are absent. |
| 2026-08-28 | **Published history rewritten and force-pushed** to remove host identifiers | accepted | User chose this over scrubbing forward only. The Proxmox IP, both subnets, the tailnet router address and the hostname were already public in `origin/main`, and the 19 unpushed commits would have added the VM address, MACs and tailnet account names. Two `git filter-repo` passes; `origin/main` is now `a820cf8`. **Accepted limitation:** this removes the hostname from the repo, it does not un-publish it. GitHub can serve unreferenced commits by SHA and existing clones keep the old data, so the name is no longer advertised but must be treated as exposed. The user declined the option that also renamed the host. |
| 2026-08-28 | **A leak scan is not evidence unless it has a positive control** | accepted | The first "history is clean" verdict was wrong. `git rev-list --all --objects` emits `sha path`, which `cat-file --batch-check` could not parse, so the scan walked 1 blob instead of 132 and reported clean. A control string known to be present exposed it, and the corrected scan found two addresses in historical `DECISIONS.md` blobs that the replacement list had missed. Generalises the standing "verify the verification" rule: a search that finds nothing and a search that cannot see are the same output. |
| 2026-08-28 | **DHCP reservations move from code to `DF_MAC_OVERRIDES` in `.env`** | accepted | `provision_vm.py` hardcoded `104: <reserved-mac>`, which is a fact about one person's LAN sitting in a public repo. Now parsed to `{vmid: mac}` with a real error for malformed input. Derived MACs are unchanged, vmid 101 still yields `BC:24:11:00:00:65`. **The env var is load-bearing:** without it a rebuilt VM 104 takes a derived MAC and drops its reservation, the exact failure that cost a session on 2026-08-27. |
| 2026-08-28 | **Cluster the existing hypervisor and the incoming second node. Explicitly not for HA** | accepted | User's call, after asking what clustering buys beyond HA. The three answers that motivated it: one UI and API across both boxes, **migration**, and replicated `/etc/pve` so storage, backup jobs, users and ACLs exist on both. Migration is the near-term driver, since it is what lets a box be opened for hardware work without downtime. Costs accepted knowingly: joining is destructive to the joiner's guests (create on the first node, join the empty second node, never the reverse), leaving effectively means a reinstall, and a two-node cluster is *worse* than two standalone hosts until the Pi qdevice lands. **Clusters do not pool RAM**, so this does nothing for the DF VM's 4096 MB ceiling. |
| 2026-08-28 | **`cpu: host` becomes `x86-64-v2-AES`** in `provision_vm.py` | accepted, not yet implemented | `cpu: host` blocks migration between different CPUs, and the two hosts are a Kaby Lake i7-7700T and a Coffee Lake i5-8500T, so the flag defeats the main reason for clustering at all. A common type fixes it; for DF the loss is negligible, it is single-threaded and not AVX-heavy. Takes effect on a cold stop/start of 104. |
| 2026-08-28 | **Script the DF install before any hardware work** | accepted | Reordering, not a new task. VM 104's install exists only as prose in `infra/local.df-vm-install.md`, which makes every hardware step a "protect 104 carefully" problem: the case opening, the possible reinstall, and the destructive cluster join. A script converts that into a twenty-minute rebuild and removes the need to back the VM up at all. It is also repo work, so it is not blocked by the tailnet the way anything touching the VM is. |
| 2026-08-28 | **`scripts/install_df.py`: the DF install is driven from the workstation over SSH, not a shell script left on the VM** | accepted, verified against VM 104 on 2026-08-28 | Matches `provision_vm.py`, reuses its `ssh_guest` transport and its `.env` discipline, and keeps the runnable artifact in the repo where it is version-controlled rather than on the box it is meant to be able to rebuild. Scripts are base64'd onto the ssh command line because two shells parse that string and the save path contains spaces (`Bay 12 Games`). Three traps from `infra/local.df-vm-install.md` are encoded as checks, not comments: the XDG save path, the silent `-gen` failure (success is decided by the region directory existing, never by exit code), and the package list. `--dry-run` prints each remote script instead of running it, which is what allowed the whole thing to be syntax- and logic-tested with the VM unreachable. **Not yet run against VM 104:** the machine is on the wrong tailnet. |
| 2026-08-28 | **A readiness check that cannot fail is not a check.** `install_df.py start` waited for `dfhack-run` to return any non-empty output | rejected, replaced | With the game down, `dfhack-run` prints `Could not connect to localhost:5000`, which is non-empty, so the wait loop broke on its first pass and the socket check ran against a sub-second-old process. A healthy start reported a 240s timeout, and the first fix attempt lengthened the timeout, which was treating the symptom and would have made the wrong diagnosis permanent in a comment. Readiness is now the socket listening AND an answer that is neither empty nor the connect error, with a positive control showing the loop runs its full deadline against a stopped game. Same lesson as the 2026-08-28 leak-scan entry, now twice in one week: **prove the check can fail before trusting that it passed.** |
| 2026-08-28 | **The systemd unit for DF must save before stopping** | implemented and verified 2026-08-30 | DF ignores SIGTERM: every stop takes the full 30s and ends in SIGKILL. A conventional unit would therefore hard-kill the fort on every host reboot. `df-fortress.service`'s `ExecStop` saves through `dfhack-run` then escalates TERM->KILL itself, `TimeoutStopSec=180`. `onboot=1` set on VM 104 via `provision_vm.py set-onboot`. A full guest reboot was triggered and both units came back with RPC answering, unattended. Host-reboot survival itself (as opposed to the guest-level proxy for it) remains untested. |
| 2026-08-30 | **`systemctl is-enabled`, never `list-unit-files \| grep -q`, for a unit-existence check under `pipefail`** | accepted | `grep -q` exits the instant it matches, SIGPIPEs the still-writing `systemctl`, and `set -o pipefail` (used throughout `install_df.py`'s remote scripts) turns that SIGPIPE into a reported pipeline failure — so `verify`'s new systemd check reported two correctly-installed, enabled units as absent. Same shape as the 2026-08-28 readiness-check entry: a check that reports failure on success is exactly as untrustworthy as one that cannot fail. The script's other `\| grep -q` uses (`swapon --show`, `ss -ltn`) are small enough single-write outputs to be much lower risk, and were left as is rather than rewritten speculatively. |
| 2026-08-30 | **SSH transport decodes remote output as UTF-8 explicitly**, not the platform default | accepted | `ssh_guest`/`scp_from` called `subprocess.run(..., text=True)` with no `encoding=`, which uses cp1252 on this Windows workstation. The first UTF-8 multibyte character in remote output — `systemctl status`'s unit-state bullet — raised `UnicodeDecodeError` and crashed the tool after the remote command had already run, discarding its result. Both now pass `encoding="utf-8", errors="replace"`; the guest is Ubuntu and everything it prints is UTF-8. Latent beyond the one command that surfaced it — any non-ASCII in remote output (a save/world name, a game error string) could have hit the same crash. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`docs/PROXMOX-SETUP.md` marked SUPERSEDED rather than deleted**, its `proxmox`/`ssd_storage` literals genericized to `<pve-node>`/`<storage>` | accepted | Its whole procedure, building a pool/role/user/token from scratch for one project, is superseded by home-lab's generalized `runbooks/project-sandbox.md`; this project is that runbook's reference instance now, and re-deriving a project-specific copy here is exactly the duplicated-truth problem this pass exists to end. Kept rather than deleted because two lessons in it cost three wrong diagnoses and are still correct PVE fact: `Sys.*` privileges are only checked at `/nodes/<node>` or `/`, never a pool or storage path, and `VM.Monitor` was removed in PVE 9. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`PVE_POOL`/`PVE_STORAGE` lose their hardcoded defaults in `scripts/pve.py`, become required via a new `_require()`** | accepted | Both defaulted to names (the old pool, the old storage ID) that stopped existing when the estate was rebuilt on 2026-09-01/06. A default that names a dead resource fails with a `403`-shaped error that reads exactly like a permissions fault, which is strictly worse than failing loudly at construction with a message that names the actual problem. `_require()` now raises immediately if either is unset. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Found and fixed same-day: `_require()` was spliced into the middle of `PVE.__init__`, leaving `self.session`'s `Authorization` header unreachable** | accepted | The refactor above added `_require()` at a point in `__init__` that left the following `self.session.headers["Authorization"] = ...` line dead code; every API call would have gone out unauthenticated and returned `401` on the very first use of the brand-new token, reading as "the new identity doesn't work" and sending the next session hunting in the ACL layer instead of the client. Caught and fixed the same day it was introduced, before any live call was made against it. Instance of the standing "verify the verification" rule: the fix was checked with a positive control, the assertion fails against the pre-fix code and passes against the fixed code, which is what makes the resulting all-clear meaningful rather than assumed. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`.gitignore` widened from `.env`/`.env.local` to `.env.*`, with `!.env.example`** | accepted | A pre-edit backup file named `.env.bak-<date>` was created untracked but not gitignored, holding an exposed Proxmox token, a hypervisor password and an API key, one `git add -A` away from being committed to this public repo. `.env` alone did not cover a sidecar with a suffix. The negation keeps a committed template possible. The sidecar has been deleted from disk. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Acknowledged: the old node label and old storage ID are also accepted-exposed identifiers, alongside the hostname** | accepted | The literals appeared in `docs/PROXMOX-SETUP.md`, in three `DECISIONS.md` rows, and across eight lines of `working-archive/Working_archive-2026-08-24.md`, meaning the 2026-08-28 redaction pass that scrubbed the hostname was incomplete, not total: it missed these two tokens. Fixed forward this session, in the current working tree, replacing the literals with `<pve-node>`/`<storage>` placeholders in all of the above. This is a forward edit landing in a new commit, not a rewrite of published git history: no history rewrite was attempted, consistent with the standing 2026-08-28 decision that force-pushing does not unpublish anything (the pre-existing commits carrying these values are treated the same way as the hostname always has been, exposed but not chased through history a second time). Recorded so a future reader does not mistake the earlier incompleteness for a fresh incident, and does not rely on an assumption that the 2026-08-28 pass was total elsewhere in the repo. |
| 2026-09-08 | **The rebuilt guest's OS hostname will carry the `.internal` suffix** | superseded, see the 2026-09-08 split row at the end — **but this row's stated reason is itself wrong, twice; do not read it as fact** | Superseded, then re-examined 2026-09-08 against home-lab directly. The original decision above ("the OS hostname carries the suffix") was **right**, and this reason retracting it is wrong on both of its claims. (1) The suffix is **not** DNS-only: home-lab's 2026-09-06 row says "an `.internal`-suffixed **OS hostname**", and `SRV-01`/`SRV-02` carry the dot in `/etc/hostname` itself. (2) There is **no zone to apply on resolution** — that same row states nothing on the LAN resolves `*.internal` and there is no local resolver, so "applied when resolving" describes machinery that does not exist. The `subnet-router-02` comparison also does not support it: that guest was built 2026-09-01, five days before the convention, and home-lab names it explicitly as an existing guest that is **not** being retrofitted — its bare hostname is a pre-convention artifact, not the rule. What was genuinely wrong in the original was narrower: the suffix belongs on the OS hostname but **not** on the Proxmox VM name field, and it cannot be delivered via `--name` at all. Kept rather than rewritten because a correction that overshot into a second wrong answer is worth being able to see. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`docs/2026-09-08-doc-reorg-plan.md` moved to `infra/local.2026-09-08-doc-reorg-plan.md`** | accepted | The file was untracked but not gitignored: one `git add -A` away from being committed to this public repo. It quotes home-lab verbatim as citations for its own claims, including the real hostname, the live LAN subnet, the host's storage path and the chassis model, so committing it would have re-leaked in one file most of what this whole pass exists to clean up. Same shape as the `.env.bak-<date>` near-miss above. Generalises past this one file: the public-repo rule applies to what a document *cites*, not only to its own subject matter, so an audit document quoting a private repo's infra specifics is itself an infra specific and belongs under `infra/local.*`, not `docs/`, regardless of its subject being documentation. "Untracked" is not "safe" in a repo where an agent may run `git add -A`. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Tarball checksums pinned and enforced in `scripts/install_df.py`**, not merely recorded | accepted | The fetch step computed a sha256 of each archive and echoed it into a log line without comparing it to anything, so `bzip2 -t` was the only real gate: that catches a truncated download or an HTML error page and proves nothing about *which* archive arrived. Bay12 serves DF over plain releases with no signature, so the hash is the only pin available. `DF_SHA256`/`DFHACK_SHA256` now carry the values measured on the 2026-08-28 install, and a mismatch refuses to unpack with a message naming the deliberate-version-bump path. The comparison deliberately runs on the cached path as well as the freshly-downloaded one, because a wrong file already sitting in `dist/` was previously trusted on sight. Third instance this session of the same shape, a check that could not fail; verified here with a positive control that exercises both the matching and mismatching cases against the shipped function. Found by the provisioning research (`research/2026-09-08-vm-provisioning.md`) while pinning the cloud image, which is the same defect one layer up. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Root `README.md` added**; this repo had never had one | accepted | User's call. The repo has been public since day one with `CLAUDE.md` as its only entry point, which serves an agent working in it and not a person arriving at it. The README states the honest status (scripted and proven, nothing currently running, no game-side code yet), leads with the commitment that actually shapes the project (the model is never shown a rendered map) rather than with the toolchain, and points into `ROADMAP.md`/`Working.md`/`decisions/`/`docs/`/`research/` instead of restating them, so it cannot drift into a fourth competing description of the same facts. Carries no infrastructure specifics, per the standing public-repo rule. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`PVE_PASSWORD` deleted from `.env`**; `PVE_USER` and `PVE_FQDN` flagged as the same shape | accepted | Verified dead on two independent grounds before removal, rather than on the assumption that it looked unused: no script reads it (the only mention in `scripts/` is a comment in `pve.py` about how the value used to break bash quoting), and the credential it named was rotated by SRV-01's 2026-09-02 OS reinstall, so the stored value had already been superseded by six days. The live equivalent is tracked in home-lab's own credential store, so nothing was lost by deleting rather than relocating it. No backup file was written, deliberately: an `.env` sidecar is exactly what caused this session's near-miss. Reading the file to delete the key also put the old value into a session transcript, so it is treated as exposed by convention even though it was already dead. |
| 2026-09-08 | **No shared provisioning library extracted yet**; restructure now, extract when a second project is named | accepted | The provisioning research made the extraction timing turn on one input, whether a second sandbox project is actually coming. Answered by the user: one will come eventually, none is planned. That is the case for the spec's own recommendation, splitting the script into generic machinery plus a per-project declaration now, so the extraction is mechanical later, while declining to invent the abstraction against a single consumer today. Guards against the failure the research names in homelab GitOps practice, where the community answer is a monorepo and the many-public-repos-plus-one-private-estate-repo split used here is not a named pattern anywhere it looked. Revisit the moment project two has a name. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`pve.py` mounts a `urllib3` retry policy**, rather than hand-rolling one | accepted | The client made every call as a single unguarded attempt while `wait_task` polls every 2s, and `fetch-image`/`clone` pass `timeout=3600`, so roughly 1800 consecutive GETs each, any one of which aborted the build. On a cluster with no QDevice that is the likeliest way a rebuild dies. Configured rather than written: `urllib3` and `requests` were already dependencies, so this is an `HTTPAdapter` mount, not new logic, which matters because the last hand-rolled change to this file left the auth header unreachable. **`allowed_methods` is left at its default on purpose, which excludes POST**: this client's POSTs create, clone, start and stop VMs, so a replayed POST after a timeout could create two VMs, while the GETs that make up the polling loop are retried. Do not add POST. `500` is deliberately absent from `status_forcelist`, because PVE returns it for real permission and quorum faults, which are persistent, and retrying those turns a clear error into a slow one. Verified with a live positive control: a GET survived two 503s and succeeded on the third, a POST reached the server exactly once. |
| 2026-09-08 | **403 and 500 responses now carry an inquorate-cluster hint** in `PVEError` | accepted | Both this repo and home-lab predicted the same misdiagnosis in writing before it could happen: an inquorate node makes `/etc/pve` read-only, so every config write fails with an error that reads exactly like an ACL fault, and the cluster currently has no QDevice with an unstable second node. No retry policy resolves it and no `status_forcelist` distinguishes it from a genuine 403, so the correct treatment is to put the possibility in front of whoever reads the error. One sentence, at the point of failure, aimed at a specific predicted wrong turn. |
| 2026-09-08 | **The template bake is deleted**; `qemu-guest-agent` moves into the guest install | accepted | The bake booted the template, installed one package over SSH, verified the agent and sealed the image again, roughly 190 lines. That package existed for one purpose: letting the API report a VM's address. Once the address is **assigned** at clone time rather than **discovered**, the agent is not needed before the guest install runs, so the package moves to `install_df.py`'s existing `PACKAGES` list and the whole apparatus goes with it: `bake_template`, `build_address`, `DF_BUILD_IP`/`_GW`/`_DNS`, `BAKE_MEMORY`, the bake memory gate, the seal, the hypervisor-side hard stop that only existed because the seal breaks graceful shutdown, and `--no-bake`. Costs, named rather than glossed: every rebuild now installs one extra package on the clone (seconds), and between clone-start and install-complete the API cannot report the VM's IP (irrelevant, the IP is assigned). Verified from Proxmox's own storage source that no tool avoids this via cloud-init, so the bake was genuinely unavoidable *as posed*; deleting it required changing the question. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Static address assigned at clone time replaces DHCP plus vmid-derived MAC pinning** | accepted | Retires the 2026-08-27 MAC-pinning row and the 2026-08-28 `DF_MAC_OVERRIDES` row: correct given DHCP, but this removes their premise. Eight mechanisms and three sources of truth (derived MAC, override table, the router's hand-edited reservation, three build-address settings, a runtime override, and the guest-agent lookup) collapse to one assignment plus one assertion. The decisive input was cross-domain, not local: Cluster API's Proxmox provider deliberately does not use DHCP, giving machines static addresses from a declared pool via cloud-init at clone. The router's reservation table was the worst part of the old design, being external, hand-edited, unreadable by any check, and not version-controlled. **`DF_VM_IP` is CIDR**, because `provision_vm.guest_address()` feeds it to `ipconfig0` which requires a prefix, and `install_df.guest_ip()` strips it for ssh: one setting, one meaning, rather than the same address in `.env` twice where the two copies can drift. |
| 2026-09-08 | **The seal survives as a written condition, now that no code enforces it** | accepted | The 2026-08-27 sealing decision (`cloud-init clean`, remove SSH host keys, truncate `machine-id` before converting a booted VM to a template) is retired as code because a template that is never booted has nothing to seal: Ubuntu cloud images ship an empty `machine-id` and no host keys, and cloud-init generates both at each clone's first boot. Recorded here and in `Working.md` because the lesson outlives the code that carried it, and the failure it prevents (every clone sharing one machine-id and host key) is silent. **Confidence is high on the mechanism and untested on this template**, which has never been built unbaked; falsify it by cloning twice and comparing host key fingerprints and `machine-id`, the same test run on 2026-08-27. If they match, the bake comes back. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Snippets cannot be uploaded through the PVE API at all**, confirmed from Proxmox's own source | accepted (fact) | `pve-storage.git`, `src/PVE/API2/Storage/Status.pm`, carries `enum => ['iso','vztmpl','import']` on **both** `upload` and `download_url`. So the `cicustom` cloud-init route is not blocked by a permission this project could be granted, it is blocked by the API surface itself, and any snippet must reach the host filesystem by some other path. This upgrades the 2026-08-27 `cicustom` rejection from inferred to verified, and simultaneously makes that whole entry moot, since the bake it justified no longer exists. Recorded as a standing PVE fact so nobody re-derives it: the answer does not change with more privileges. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`ciupgrade` and `arch` are `root@pam`-only VM parameters** | accepted (fact) | Unreachable by any pool-scoped token regardless of role, the same class as the `migration_type` restriction home-lab already records. Practical consequence for this project: leave both unset. Noted because a denial on either would read as a broken ACL and send someone back into the identity layer, which is the recurring misdiagnosis this repo keeps paying for. |
| 2026-09-08 | **PVE `download-url` takes `checksum` and `checksum-algorithm`** | accepted (fact) | Confirmed from two independent primary sources. Means the base image can be pinned by hash and verified **host-side**, with no host shell and no post-download check of our own, which is the missing half of image pinning: a dated URL is a name, the hash is the pin. Usable today and independent of the build-tool decision. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Ansible's own documentation contradicts itself on WSL as a control node** | accepted (fact) | The installation guide lists "Windows under a Windows Subsystem for Linux (WSL) distribution" as supported; the Windows OS guide says "The Windows Subsystem for Linux is not supported by Ansible and should not be used for production systems." Recorded so this is never re-litigated from whichever page someone lands on first. It also corrects two errors in one: an earlier research spec presented the restrictive page as settled fact, and the correction of that spec then called the claim simply false. Both were wrong. The honest status is contested. |
| 2026-09-08 | **The 2026-08-24 rejection of Terraform is formally reopened**, conditionally | proposed | That entry rejected Terraform while naming `bpg/proxmox` as the provider to revisit if it were ever reconsidered, so this is the sanctioned revisit rather than a re-litigation. Its stated reason (the Telmate provider and `VM.Monitor`) never applied to `bpg`. Two objections raised more recently were both checked against primary sources and are false: `bpg` documents SSH as **not** required for creating, modifying or deleting VMs or managing storage, and its `import_from` accepts a `<datastore>:import/<file>` volume, which is exactly this project's existing pattern. **Status is deliberately `proposed`, not accepted:** whether a pool-scoped token can actually drive it end to end is unverified and is what the spike settles. → `research/2026-09-08-provisioning-recommendation.md` §7. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Guest OS stays Ubuntu 24.04 LTS**, and the image is pinned to a dated serial with a sha256 | accepted | User's call, after asking whether Ubuntu was too heavy. It is not, in any way that matters here: DF's measured worldgen peak is 561 MB in a VM sized 6144/2048 with a 25 G disk, so the base image is rounding error, and the lever on weight would be DF and the Xvfb stack rather than the distro. The real axis is churn, and Debian's edge there (no snapd, slower stable) is smaller than the cost of re-verifying the `ldd ./dwarfort` package list, which was proven against this exact distro. Churn is addressed directly instead, see the row below. **Pinning:** `noble/current/` was a moving target with no checksum, which made "rebuildable" true and "reproducible" false. Now `releases/noble/release-20260826/` plus `checksum`/`checksum-algorithm` on `download-url`, so PVE verifies host-side with no host shell. Two traps confirmed against the published tree: the filename differs between the daily and releases trees (`ubuntu-24.04-server-cloudimg-amd64.img`, and `noble-server-cloudimg-amd64.img` is absent there), so changing only the directory 404s; and the daily tree keeps about six serials where releases goes back to 2024. **The serial is also in the destination filename**, because the "already present" check matches on volid, so without it a bumped pin would silently reuse the old image: the cached-file trust bug that bit the DF tarballs earlier the same day, in a different costume. |
| 2026-09-08 | **Automatic upgrades are disabled on the fortress VM** | accepted | The same call the register already made on 2026-08-26, one layer down. DF Classic replaced the Steam build because an auto-update mid-fort shifts memory offsets and silently breaks DFHack; an unattended glibc or SDL2 upgrade under a live fort is that identical hazard, and a fort is meant to run unattended for a month. Written as apt config (`/etc/apt/apt.conf.d/99-df-overseer-no-auto-upgrade`) rather than by masking the unit, because config survives the package being updated. **The security tradeoff is real and accepted knowingly:** the containment is that this VM is LAN-only and cheap to rebuild, which is the project's whole thesis. The intended pattern is to pin the box for the duration of a fort and rebuild between forts, not to let libraries move under a running one. If that pattern is ever abandoned, revisit this. |
| 2026-09-08 | **`PVE_FQDN` and `PVE_USER` deleted from `.env` too**, leaving 15 keys, all live | accepted | Closes the row above, which flagged both as dead but left them in place. Confirmed unreferenced across every tracked file and every gitignored `infra/local.*` record, not just `scripts/`. The prompt was the user editing `PVE_FQDN` to a new hostname and asking whether that was right: the edit did nothing at all, because the client builds its base URL from `PVE_HOST`. **That is the cost of a dead setting, and the argument for deleting rather than merely flagging them:** a key that looks live but is read by nothing invites a change that appears to work and silently does not. Related and worth knowing before anyone tries it properly: the name in question does not resolve from the workstation, so pointing `PVE_HOST` at it would have failed at DNS. Moving off a hardcoded address is still worth doing, and needs a hosts entry or the resolver serving that zone first. |

The `checksum`/`checksum-algorithm` parameters on `download-url` are spelled correctly and PVE verified the image host-side, which was the least-confident claim in `research/2026-09-08-provisioning-recommendation.md` and the one it explicitly flagged for a probe before anything depended on it. The dedicated storage does carry the `import` content type, the one-time human step a pool-scoped token cannot perform. The token drives the entire image-to-template flow with no host shell. And **the bake deletion works end to end**: the template was created, imported, resized and converted without ever being booted, so there was nothing to seal. Note the disk resize succeeded on attempt 1 of 4, so the 2026-08-27 storage-flush timeout did not recur here; the retry stays, since one clean run does not disprove an intermittent fault. `net0` came out with a Proxmox-random MAC as expected now that vmid-derived pinning is gone, which is harmless because the address is assigned at clone time. **Consequence beyond this repo: home-lab's Phase H is discharged**, so the old exposed `df-overseer@pve` token is now retirable. |

Removed at a root shell on a node confirmed `Quorate` first, because an inquorate node makes `/etc/pve` read-only and would have failed in a way that reads like a permissions fault: token `api`, then the user, then the `DFOverseer` and `DFOverseerNode` roles. **Checking before deleting paid for itself twice:** the old user held a `/sdn/zones/localnetwork` grant that appears nowhere in this repo's records, and a `/nodes/proxmox` binding pointing at a node name that stopped existing on 2026-09-02. Deleting the user took all three ACLs with it, and nothing was lost because the new identity already holds `PVESDNUser` on that zone. **Verified in both directions**, that the old user, its ACLs and both roles are gone, and that `svc-df-overseer-sandbox@pve` still exists, since a check that only proves the deletion worked would not catch deleting the wrong one; `grep df-overseer` matches both names, which is the trap here. `provision_vm.py status` was re-run afterwards and still authenticates. |

| 2026-09-08 | **VM rebuilt: `clone` produced VM 103 at `<df-vm-ip>`, started and reachable over SSH** | accepted, verified | User supplied `DF_VM_IP=<df-vm-ip>/24`. `clone --name df-fortress --full` (full clone, matching the 2026-08-24 precedent for VM 104) built VM 103 from template 102, `start --vmid 103` brought it up within host memory headroom, and `install_df.py verify --vmid 103` reached it over SSH as the cloud-init user (`DF_CIUSER`, default `df`) with no address discovery or bake. | 2026-09-08 | **VM 103 renamed `df-fortress` → `df-colony-01`, correcting the `.internal` misread above** | accepted, verified | Two problems, caught by the user: "df-fortress" is redundant ("df" already means Dwarf Fortress), and `--name df-fortress` put the literal string `.internal` into the Proxmox name field, unlike `subnet-router-02`'s naming where the suffix is DNS-only. Checked the guest before fixing it: `hostname`/`/etc/hostname` already read the short form `df-fortress`, not the FQDN, so cloud-init had already done the right thing at first boot and the bug was cosmetic to Proxmox's own name field only. Fixed both: `PUT .../config {"name": "df-colony-01"}` on the Proxmox side, `hostnamectl set-hostname df-colony-01` plus the `/etc/hosts` `127.0.1.1` line on the guest side, verified by reading both back. `DF_VMID=103` in `.env` is unaffected; nothing in `scripts/` keys off the VM name. |

This replaces both earlier misreads in one step: not "hostname carries it" alone (the first row above), and not "neither field carries it" either (this session's intermediate read, corrected by the user mid-session). `scripts/install_df.py` gained `step_hostname`, run first in `cmd_install` (now `[1/6]`, the rest renumbered): reads the VM's Proxmox name via the API (skipped under `--dry-run`, matching `target()`'s existing no-live-call contract), sets `hostnamectl` and the `/etc/hosts` `127.0.1.1` line to `<name>.internal`, idempotent like every other step. Verified by hand against VM 103 before being scripted, then scripted so a future rebuild does not require doing this by hand again. |

This is the exact trap Working.md already named (manual vs systemd paths contending for :99), now with the full failure mode: an orphan outliving a manual session poisons every later systemd-managed start. Caught by the user ("i suspect u added the new password on the same line as the anthropic key"), fixed by splitting the merged line back into two, and verified clean by loading through the actual `pve.load_env()` parser (not by eyeballing the file) — `DF_VNC_PASSWORD` came back 20 chars with no `ANTHROPIC` substring, `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` came back 108 chars starting `sk-ant`, matching a real key shape. | 2026-09-09 | **Public leg goes over Cloudflare Tunnel (`cloudflared` on the relay), not `research/2026-09-09-reverse-vnc-relay.md` §6's generic-VPS/nginx/Let's Encrypt sketch** — `provision_relay.py cloudflared` installs it, outbound-only, TLS terminates at Cloudflare's edge, origin is `http://localhost:6080` (the already-running `df-webvnc.service`), public hostname `dwarf-fortress.willsmith.nz` | accepted, verified (install only — token pending) | **Explicit, deliberate simplification of that research doc, not a silent deviation from it.** §6 was written assuming a generic VPS with its own public IP, an open inbound 443, and certbot-managed TLS, because at the time no concrete public-hosting mechanism had been chosen. Cloudflare Tunnel changes the premise: `cloudflared` dials out to Cloudflare's edge, so the relay opens **no inbound port at all**, needs no public IP, and needs no certificate of its own — strictly less infrastructure than §6 scoped, for the same result. Install source verified live against `pkg.cloudflare.com`'s own index page (curl'd directly, not paraphrased): the current documented method is a `/usr/share/keyrings/` keyring file plus an inline `signed-by=` apt line, not the deprecated `apt-key` path. `cloudflared 2026.8.3` is installed and idempotently verified running on the relay (`<relay-vm-ip>`). Confirmed against current Cloudflare docs, not assumed: the dashboard's token-based install (`cloudflared service install <token>`) is the documented non-interactive flow for a dashboard-managed tunnel, and adding a Public Hostname in that dashboard auto-creates the DNS CNAME when the zone is already on Cloudflare DNS (true for `willsmith.nz`) — no separate `cloudflared tunnel route dns` step, which belongs to the older CLI-managed tunnel workflow this project isn't using. **Blocked on the one thing this repo cannot do for itself:** the dashboard connector token, which only exists once a human creates the tunnel in the Zero Trust UI. | 2026-09-11 | **Root-caused the quicksave "silent no-op": it's an async, render-loop-gated overlay, not a synchronous save call — the two apparent no-ops were most likely just longer lag, not a distinct failure** | accepted (root cause), **downgrades the two rows above from "confirmed silent no-op" to "unresolved, likely lag"** | Dispatched a researcher subagent after the user was right to be suspicious of an earlier claimed no-op.
