Live-reproduced under quiet conditions: one call returned exit 0 with no log trace for over 45 seconds before the save landed for real; a second reproduction completed in 8 seconds, so the delay is genuinely variable, likely contention on DFHack's single scripting thread. This falsifies the project's prior assumption that no log line within a few dozen seconds meant no-op. The two historical no-ops' true fate is now unrecoverable, downgraded to unresolved-likely-lag. New protocol: poll the active slot's mtime for up to ~90 seconds. → memory/fort-operations-and-incidents.md#2026-09-11-root-cause-of-quicksave-silent-no-op-async-render-loop-gated-overlay |
| 2026-09-11 | **Fixed a real, latent risk in both DF stop paths: a flat `sleep 5` between quicksave and SIGTERM, predating the async-quicksave root-cause above, could have SIGTERM'd `dwarfort` mid-write** | accepted, verified live | Re-reading `systemd-stop.sh` (the live `ExecStop`) and `install_df.py`'s manual `stop --save` while preparing to stop VM 103 for the `set-cpu` row below surfaced that both still assumed a save completes within 5 seconds — the exact assumption the row above had already falsified (45-80s+ observed). Fixed both to poll the active slot's `world.sav` mtime (re-reading `cur_savegame.save_dir` fresh each poll, since quicksave rotates slots forward) for up to 90s before sending SIGTERM, instead of trusting a fixed delay or `stderr.log`'s unreliable "should autosave" line. Verified live, for real, stopping VM 103 for the `set-cpu` attempt below: journal showed `quicksave confirmed on disk (slot: autosave 2)` at +10s, then the existing 30s TERM-wait loop ran its full course before a SIGKILL was actually needed — **DF still genuinely ignores SIGTERM**, reconfirmed, and the new poll logic caught the real completion correctly rather than cutting in early or firing blind. Also added `provision_vm.py shutdown` (ACPI), the missing VM-level counterpart to `cmd_start` — needed since a guest-level DF stop doesn't power off the VM itself, which `set-cpu` needs to actually take effect. |

| 2026-09-12 | **`openclaw` is the sole agent host. `hermes-agent` is not used, including for the Overseer** | accepted | User considered putting the Overseer on `hermes-agent` specifically because it was believed to survive longest. Second and decisive, the structural argument: **an Overseer outage is not fatal.** A fort that makes no decisions for six hours is fine; a fort with no watchdog is not. | 2026-09-12 | **Multi-agent architecture designed: one actor (Overseer), read-only specialist advisors, two code-level components (Sentry, Triage)** | accepted as design, **nothing built** | Full design in `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md`, written from a design conversation this session. Roster: Overseer (sole actor, arbitration/priority/plan/playbooks), Architect, Quartermaster, Marshal, Consultant, Chronicler. Specialists are read-only and may call exactly one write tool, `propose`. The governing test applied to every candidate role was "if it is computable, it is a tool; an agent exists only where there is judgment under uncertainty", which is design commitment #2 restated. Cost is controlled by Triage: code reads the diff each heartbeat and a quiet cycle costs zero tokens, which matters because a resident roster on a fort at `FPS_CAP:5` would burn money producing nothing (the compliance eval's $9-13 afternoon is the cautionary data point). |
| 2026-09-12 | **Two proposed roles rejected as agents and moved into code**: efficiency-gap analysis, and a safety veto over fort-enders | accepted | User proposed both as agents. Efficiency analysis (idle counts, stalled jobs, hauling distance, unlinked stockpiles) is deterministic analysis over structured state, so an LLM doing it is nondeterministic and expensive at something code does exactly; it becomes a tool returning ranked findings, extending the pattern `get_stuck_jobs`/`find_open_area` already set. Fort-enders (aquifer breach, magma, unsealed caverns, atom-smashing a live dwarf) become **refusals in the tool layer**, because a guardrail that depends on an agent remembering to check is not a guardrail. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Single writer in v1. If throughput ever justifies it, escalate to single-writer-per-resource, not to multiple general writers** | accepted | DF offers no transaction boundary, so two actors that can both designate and both assign labor corrupt each other's work; preventing that means building reservations and locking, which costs more than a serial decider buys at five frames per second. User asked the right question, whether DF commands slice cleanly, and named cursor state as the suspected obstacle: correct. `quickfort run -c x,y,z` and direct DFHack calls are cleanly sliceable; the UI path (`df-overseer-ui`, `xdotool`) depends on one keyboard, one mouse, one focused screen and can never be. Other crossed dependencies: overlapping designations, `autolabor` as a global policy writer already live on the fort, burrows, the manager work-order queue's ordering, pause state, `dfhack.persistent`. **Quartermaster (work orders plus stockpile settings) is the designated first carve-out candidate**, and allowlists are to be designed so that carve is a config change. Decisive argument against carving now: carving only pays for a role that is both urgent and frequent, and playbooks already gave the urgent path its own fast lane. Being settled empirically rather than by argument in `research/2026-09-12-write-conflict-matrix.md`, and mechanically gated on whether concurrent `dfhack-run` calls are safe at all. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Reaction latency is answered by precommitment (playbooks executed by code), not by adding actors. One agent per squad is rejected** | accepted | An agent round trip is tens of seconds; a precompiled reflex is milliseconds. DF combat resolves in seconds, so no agent conversation can ever sit inside a fight, and everything that actually decides a fight happens before it (burrows, stationing, equipment, training, the bridge) or after it (hospital, recovery). The Marshal's deliverable is therefore **playbooks, not live orders**. Compounding reason: `unit-status hostile` is verified unreliable in both directions (2026-09-11), so a squad command hierarchy built on it would be confidently wrong on a schedule. Playbooks must be **structured data, not prose**, so thresholds are tunable and therefore learnable; and no reflex retunes itself, changes go through the normal proposal queue. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Sentry response is graded (reflex / throttle / pause / alert), not binary pause. A pause must expire** | accepted | User's objection: a frequently frozen fort is bad to watch, and the fort is an ambient display and public stream. Pausing is nonetheless the strongest latency lever available (a fort at `FPS_CAP:5` loses nothing while paused), so it is kept but demoted to rare, budgeted and logged, reserved for situations both urgent and not precompilable. Throttling (lowering the frame cap to buy thinking time without freezing) is the preferred middle setting but is **unverified**: whether DFHack can change the cap at runtime is question 1 of `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-capability-checks.md`. Two safety notes: pausing is the safe direction while resuming is the sensitive one, and a Sentry that pauses needs a dead man's handle (auto-resume on timeout plus an alert), because a fort frozen indefinitely by a crashed Overseer quietly defeats "runs unattended" and is visible on the public feed. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Agents communicate only through one append-only queue. Writes are tool calls with typed fields, reads are rendered as XML. No peer-to-peer specialist chat in v1** | accepted, the no-chat half pending evidence | Making the queue the channel rather than a log of the channel means an unaudited communication is structurally impossible, which is the cheapest way to get the auditability the user requires. A specialist cannot emit prose: it calls `propose(...)`, validated at write time, so malformed proposals are refused rather than becoming a parsing problem later. A proposal's `type` must come from a **closed vocabulary**, otherwise every proposal is bespoke, no two are the same kind of decision, and no hit rate can ever accumulate, which would silently defeat the whole calibration scheme. Peer chat is excluded because free-form agent chat scales cost faster than value and propagates errors (an LLM treats a peer's confident assertion as evidence), but this is flagged as the most likely thing in the design to be wrong and is the load-bearing question of `research/2026-09-12-multi-agent-architecture-prior-art.md`. |
| 2026-09-12 | **One snapshot per cycle, many per-role projections. Three information tiers, earned** | accepted | User asked whether specialists should all get the same information. The resolution: a shared snapshot is not a shared briefing. Consistency comes from one canonical, tick-stamped read (without it, the Architect reading at t=0 and the Marshal at t=12s write proposals about two different worlds, and the Overseer cannot tell whether a disagreement is judgment or timing; the concrete failure is accepting both a room and a seal on the same corridor). Economy comes from narrow projections. Tiers: 0 signals (numbers only, O(1) in fort size), 1 role briefing, 2 detail on request only. The governing constraint is that **prompt size must not scale with fortress size**, since a fort at 100 dwarves has many times the state of one at 15. Snapshots also make per-role model choice an eval rather than a guess, because a snapshot plus its proposals is a replayable test case. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Confidence in facts is stated by the tool; confidence in proposals is measured from graded predictions. Self-reported confidence is never a decision input** | accepted | User wanted agents to grade confidence and learn what deserves it. Asking a model for a confidence number would have produced a field that looks rigorous and predicts nothing (this register's 2026-08-25 row already records that LLM self-assessment is largely uncorrelated with truth). Instead, three layers. Tool outputs carry a reliability tag extending `learning/ledger`'s existing `field_source` vocabulary with `HEURISTIC` alongside `MECHANICAL`/`DERIVED`, so agents inherit reliability rather than inventing it, and a correction lands in one line of `TOOLS.yaml` instead of in six agents' memories; `unit-status hostile` is the first evidenced `HEURISTIC` entry. Proposals carry falsifiable predictions graded mechanically against the ledger. The confidence that then matters is the **measured historical hit rate per role and per proposal type**, which the Overseer can weight by. Self-reported confidence may be captured as a field to be graded, never trusted until proven calibrated. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Reflex firings are logged with cost and a follow-up check, and the reflex set is kept deliberately small** | accepted | User's call, and a good one: reflexes are the ideal learning subject because unlike most fortress decisions they are discrete, repeated and near-identical, so they accumulate N samples. What the log genuinely yields: cost accounting (work stopped, dwarves locked out, caravan turned away), false-positive rate, and trigger tuning. **The reflex log is also the dataset that repairs the threat sensor**, which is currently the project's worst-known signal, and that alone justifies building it. What it cannot yield, stated so it is not overclaimed later: whether a reflex ever saved the fortress, which needs a counterfactual, which rests on DF replay determinism, which is unverified. Since benefit is unprovable and cost is measurable, the reflex set stays small and cost-capped, or the fort accumulates expensive superstitions. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Agent role definitions live in this repo; runtime and provider config stays outside** | accepted | Design commitment #5 says the brain lives outside this repo, which raised a real question once a roster exists. Split: charters, tool allowlists, eval suites, playbook schema and queue schema are in this repo, because they are about the DF domain and this repo's own tool surface and they port unchanged to any MCP-speaking brain, which is the entire point of the boundary. API keys, provider config and `openclaw`'s runtime wiring stay out. Consistent with the 2026-09-12 reasoning already recorded against hand-fitting one brain's conventions ahead of the MCP seam existing. Modularity mechanism: one directory per role plus a single `ROSTER.yaml`, so adding or removing a role is a directory and one line, never a code change. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Publish the structured proposal/decision stream to the public feed, not raw agent thinking** | proposed, needs explicit go-ahead before it ships | User's idea, and it fits the project's own stated position that the distinctive content is the reasoning beside the image rather than the image. Raw chain-of-thought is rejected on two grounds: it is long, repetitive and poor viewing, and it is where accidents live, since tool errors carry file paths and stack traces carry host details, on a public page belonging to a project that keeps infrastructure specifics out of a public repo on purpose. The queue is already structured, so the public feed is a rendering of it at near-zero extra cost, plus one model-authored `public_rationale` field written deliberately for an audience. Three conditions: **allowlist the published fields, never regex-redact a firehose** (a denylist fails silently, and silent failure on a public page cannot be walked back); delay by 30-60s so a filter can run and a kill switch exists; publish Sentry state too, so a throttle or pause reads as visible deliberation rather than a crash. Publishing is outward-facing, so it gets its own row and its own go-ahead at the time. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Found and verified: `set_labor` already races `autolabor` on ordinary citizens, and `ROADMAP.md`'s claim that autolabor "doesn't blanket-override the manual primitives" was an overstatement** | accepted as a finding, **fix not yet designed** | Surfaced by the write-conflict audit (`research/2026-09-12-write-conflict-matrix.md`) and then confirmed at primary source rather than taken on the subagent's word: `df-overseer-labor.lua`'s `set_labor` writes `unit.status.labors[code]` directly (line ~196) with no coordination of any kind, and `autolabor` (enabled on the live fort 2026-09-11, confirmed actually assigning jobs) reassigns labors on its own cycle, exempting only military-duty and burrow-restricted units. So a `set_labor` call on an ordinary citizen is an unprotected race that autolabor will probably win on its next pass. The exemption is real, which is what the earlier claim was reading, but it protects a narrow subset and not "the manual primitives" generally. Recorded rather than silently patched, per this repo's docs-versus-reality rule; `ROADMAP.md`'s parenthetical is corrected in place with a pointer here. Notable because it is a **single-writer violation that already exists in production**, before any agent roster is built, which is incidental corroboration of the single-writer decision made the same day. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Found: the Quartermaster and Chronicler roles have essentially no tool surface, and the Quartermaster hypothesis therefore cannot be tested yet** | accepted as a finding | Same audit. 18 of 25 existing subcommands are pure reads; only 7 mutate (3 via `quickfort -c`, 1 via a direct labor struct write, 3 via the UI input path). Neither manager work orders nor stockpile *settings* (filters, thresholds, links) have any tool surface anywhere in this repo, and the stock DFHack tools that would normally cover it (`stocks`, `workflow`) are tagged `unavailable` on this install. So the "work orders plus stockpile settings is a disjoint write domain" hypothesis is currently untestable rather than confirmed or refuted, and it carries a plausible hidden coupling: a working Quartermaster will want to reassign labor when a threshold trips, and the only labor tool that exists is the one that already races `autolabor` (row above). Chronicler has nothing at all: no tool in this repo writes a chronicle entry, and the nearest analog, `df-overseer-diff.lua`'s event log, is explicitly ephemeral, which is the opposite of what a chronicle needs. Also found: two read-only tools drain from or register into a single unpartitioned `_G` table shared by every caller, a shared-resource hazard for concurrent readers even with one writer. |
| 2026-09-12 | **"No peer-to-peer specialist chat" confirmed by prior art, with one doctrine-backed relaxation identified for v2: read-only cross-advisor visibility without cross-advisor authority** | accepted, the v2 relaxation recorded as an option not adopted | `research/2026-09-12-multi-agent-architecture-prior-art.md`. The ban was flagged the same day as the design's most-likely-wrong call, so it was tested rather than assumed. Convergent multi-source evidence upheld it: two independent production postmortems both arrived at hierarchical orchestrator-to-worker with no worker-to-worker channel; a failure taxonomy over 1,600+ real multi-agent traces with validated inter-annotator agreement attributes roughly a third of multi-agent LLM failures to inter-agent misalignment, the exact class peer chat enables; and a debate-failure paper supplies the mechanism, peers negotiating one shared answer drift toward conformity more often than correctness. The debate literature's apparent counter-evidence resolves *for* this design: debate helps for many independent attempts arbitrated afterward, which is what a single decider with advisors already is, and hurts for peers jointly negotiating one answer. Two honest qualifications recorded rather than glossed: the evidence dates 2023-2025 and says "not now with today's coordination patterns", not "never"; and incident command doctrine explicitly permits read-only information exchange between peers while keeping authority singular, which is the considered v2 relaxation (see another's proposal, still may not negotiate with it or act). → `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §4. |
| 2026-09-12 | **This design is a blackboard system, and it had rebuilt only one third of one. Adopting the missing piece: an explicit advisor scheduler, plus a span-of-control ceiling** | accepted | Same brief, and the single most useful reframe it produced. A modern multi-agent stack that gives agents a shared workspace has reimplemented the shared-workspace third of the classic blackboard architecture and discarded two others: credibility-weighted contributions, and an explicitly engineered scheduler deciding who acts next rather than round-robin or free-for-all. Audit against this design: the queue is the workspace (have it), the tool reliability tags plus measured track record are the credibility weighting (have it, via measurement rather than self-report), the four record types are the hierarchical typed store (have it, corroborated), and **the scheduler was implicit in prompt order, which is the gap**. Now a named concern inside Triage. Two constraints on it: the economics differ from a classical blackboard, whose knowledge sources could cheaply ask "am I relevant" many times, whereas an LLM advisor cannot be polled that cheaply, so the real question is coarser ("which one or two advisors are worth their token cost this cycle"); and incident command's roughly-five span of control, which the six-role roster happens to match, treated as a starting guess to tune. Sharpened by a published multi-agent research system measuring roughly 15x single-agent token cost, with token usage explaining most performance variance and the approach performing **worse on tightly interdependent tasks**, which fortress management moderately is. The roster is therefore not free and not self-evidently correct; the scheduler is what keeps it honest. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Calibration method chosen: Brier score plus a coarse reliability diagram now, track-record weighting next, reference-class forecasting and formal recalibration curves explicitly deferred** | accepted | Same brief, which was asked specifically which methods are affordable at the data scale one fortress produces rather than which are best in principle. Brier scoring fits the planned ledger as it already exists. Track-record weighting (scaling an advisor's future influence by its historical score) is the mechanism by which earned confidence, and only earned confidence, reaches a decision, and it sits closer to the well-replicated basic-feedback-loop finding than to heavy aggregation machinery. Reference-class forecasting and formal recalibration curves are strong in their home domains but need far more history than this project will have for a long time, so they are recorded as deferred rather than quietly built. **Resolves an apparent contradiction with the same day's no-self-reported-confidence row**: Brier scoring requires a stated probability, so advisors do attach one, but it is **scored, not obeyed**, and scoring it is precisely how we learn whether it deserves to be obeyed. Also satisfies the blackboard credibility-weighting element by measurement rather than self-report. |
| 2026-09-12 | **openclaw verified to support per-agent models AND per-agent tool scoping as real features. The roster's enforcement mechanism exists** | accepted | `research/2026-09-12-openclaw-primitives.md`. This was the design's single biggest risk: principle 8 says a role is defined by its tool allowlist, which is unenforceable if the host cannot scope tools per agent, and §11's whole modularity scheme rests on it. It cleared. Named agents under `agents.entries.<id>`, each with its own model as `"provider/model"` and its own workspace; scoping via three real mechanisms (`tools.allow/deny` per agent, `sandbox.workspaceAccess`, and MCP-level `toolFilter.include/exclude` plus per-server agent allowlists), not workarounds. MCP servers are declared globally and scoped to agents, over stdio or HTTP. Read-only specialists are therefore directly expressible. Per-agent persistence is a per-agent SQLite store not shared between agents, which is consistent with the standing decision to keep `learning/` as plain JSONL behind the MCP seam rather than in host-native memory. Diligence worth noting: the brief flagged SEO content farms and an unrelated same-named project, and traced every claim to the project's own repository and docs. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Four openclaw constraints the design must accommodate, one of which invalidates a claim made in the design a few hours earlier** | accepted as constraints | Same brief. (1) **No hard spend cap exists in the host**, only context-size limits and cost visibility, so §11's per-role budget ceiling cannot be enforced by openclaw and must be enforced by our own Triage gating plus provider-side billing caps. Most important operationally, given this project already overran $9-13 in one afternoon on an eval. (2) **Fan-out is not free**: agent concurrency is lane-based and the inter-agent send lane is serialised at one concurrent operation, roughly 30 seconds per target, so waking five specialists may cost minutes. The design's "specialists run in parallel" is now stated as conditional: read-only advisors are always *safe* to run concurrently, a correctness property, but not necessarily *cheap*, which promotes the §4 scheduler from optimisation to requirement. (3) **The only true external push wake is an authenticated HTTP hooks endpoint**, everything else being heartbeat or cron with a 30-second floor on condition watchers: that is the concrete mechanism the Sentry needed to reach the Overseer and previously lacked. (4) **Host crash recovery covers its own conversational state, not external side effects**, with nothing found addressing whether a partially-applied fortress mutation is detectable afterward, which makes the write-ahead queue the actual recovery mechanism rather than belt-and-braces, with this project's existing quicksave-checkpoint discipline as the real safety net. Also useful: tool-call logs are machine-parseable JSONL with correlating ids, so the mechanical friction log is buildable, though the host's own redaction is best-effort, which is exactly why the public feed allowlists fields and never renders host logs. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Correction: the 2026-08-25 claim that openclaw's scheduler has exponential retry backoff does not hold for the heartbeat path** | accepted as a correction, **reported by brief, not independently confirmed here** | `research/2026-09-12-openclaw-primitives.md` cites two issues, described as closed-not-fixed, documenting linear or uncapped retry storms on heartbeat specifically. Recorded with that provenance rather than restated as verified, since this repo's rule is to check a blocking claim at primary source before repeating it and the primary sources here are external issue trackers that were not opened from this session. Consequence: treat heartbeat retry as unsafe by default. Worth stating plainly that **the same-day sole-host decision cited this exact property in its reasoning**, so part of that row's supporting argument is now wrong; the decision itself does not turn on it, because the decisive argument was structural (an Overseer outage is not fatal, and reliability belongs in code under systemd), not a scheduler comparison. Noted because the 2026-08-25 row already records one wrong confident claim about a scheduler difference, and this is a second in the same area: scheduler-quality claims about either candidate host should be treated as unreliable unless tested. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Concurrent `dfhack-run` is safe but tick-gated and unordered, which kills the throughput argument for partitioning write authority. Single writer is now the settled design, not a v1 simplification** | accepted | `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-capability-checks.md` §3, settled from DFHack's actual C++ synchronisation primitive read at the matching version tag rather than inferred. The RPC server genuinely is multi-threaded (a thread per client connection, so the naive "single console" model is wrong), but every touch of game state passes through one `std::recursive_timed_mutex` (`Core::CoreSuspendMutex`) via `CoreSuspender`, so concurrent writers cannot corrupt fortress state: memory safety was never the risk. What they do not get is throughput, ordering or low latency. It is a lock with cooperative hand-off, not a FIFO queue, so `recursive_timed_mutex`'s lack of fairness means service order among waiting writers is neither deterministic nor priority-orderable from outside; and the suspend window opens only **once per simulation tick**, after the tick's own work. Therefore **every writer serialises against every other writer and against the tick**, and more writers means each waits longer. The throughput case for carving, already weak because playbooks handle urgency, is dead. The only remaining reasons to carve are organisational, and none outweigh losing the single coherent plan, the unfragmented WIP limit and the ordered audit narrative. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Source-confirmed the root cause of the previously-unexplained 45-80s command-to-effect delay**: DFHack's suspend window opens once per simulation tick | accepted, upgrades an earlier finding's confidence | Same brief. `research/2026-09-11-quicksave-silent-noop.md` measured the delay live and could only call the mechanism "the most parsimonious, evidence-consistent explanation... not independently confirmed". Reading `Core::Update()` and the `CoreWakeup.wait()` hand-off confirms it directly. That report's own confidence framing should be upgraded from plausible to confirmed. Recorded separately from the row above because it is a distinct, already-open question being closed, not a new design call, and because it is a reusable fact about this whole platform: **any command's latency is bounded below by the tick rate**, which is why the same mechanism also makes the throttle tier a latency trap (row below). |
| 2026-09-12 | **The throttle tier is real but not free: lowering the frame cap also slows every agent tool call, because the suspend window is tick-gated** | accepted, **and it may invert the tier's rationale, pending measurement** | Runtime frame-cap control is verified (`setfps.lua` writes `df.global.enabler.fps`; persists in-process across map loads, not across a process restart), so the tier exists. But since DFHack opens its suspend window once per tick, a lower cap means tool calls wait for a boundary that arrives less often: throttling trades game speed for **agent** latency rather than buying thinking time outright. It follows, though this is reasoning and not yet verified, that **pausing may beat throttling for tool-call throughput** if suspend windows keep opening at render rate while the simulation is halted, which would invert the intuition the tier was built on. The capability brief did not establish whether `Core::Update` still runs while paused. Recorded as a measure-before-relying item rather than acted on, because the fix depends on which way that goes. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The wake vocabulary has real signals for only five of nine entries. `breach` has none at all, and `hostile_detected` is structurally blind to everything but registered invasions** | accepted as a finding, **two detectors are now required build work** | Same brief, checked against DFHack's real `EventType` enum, `eventful`'s actual Lua bindings, the `announcement_type` enum, and the live install's own `announcements.txt` for which categories are enabled. Real: `cave_in`, `migrant_wave`, `caravan_arrived`, `season_change` (all via the generic `REPORT` event filtered by announcement type, each confirmed default-enabled) and `unit_critical` (via `UNIT_ATTACK`, which hands back a real wound struct, though "badly" is our own logic over it). `job_stalled` and `stock_below_threshold` were always ours, not events. **`breach` has no signal of any kind**, a checked negative: no event type and no announcement type matches, with `df.global.world.flows` plus map-block liquid scanning as the nearest unconfirmed polling target. **`hostile_detected` has only `INVASION`**, which fires when DF registers an actual invasion and not for an ambush, a lone thief, a sneaking creature, or wildlife turning aggressive. That is independent, source-level corroboration of the 2026-09-11 kea finding: the blind spot is not one unreliable polling signal, it is **the event layer too, by construction**. Consequences: the Marshal's playbooks cannot rest on either hostile signal, so a purpose-built detector is required work rather than recalibration; and until a breach poller exists, **flood response is not covered by anything in the design**, which matters because breach is among the fastest fort-killers. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Work-order priority is list position, not a number. DF's 1-7 applies to dig designations only** | accepted | Same brief. The `manager_order` struct was read in full and has **no priority field**; priority is realised purely as position in the ordered `world.manager_orders.all` vector. `quickfort`'s `#dig` blueprints do accept `-p 1-7`, so the 1-7 mechanism the user asked about is real for digging. The design consequence is small but would have caused a confusing bug: the Overseer's "set priority" action is a number in one case and a list insertion in the other, and the proposal schema's `suggested_priority` field must not pretend they are the same mechanism. Also found, and a genuine correction in emphasis to `research/2026-09-12-write-conflict-matrix.md`: **`workorder.lua` is present and callable** with a real `create_orders()`, so while this repo has no work-order tool, the Quartermaster has a real path to one; `stocks` and `workflow` being unavailable does not block it. |
| 2026-09-12 | **`dfhack.persistent` constraints: no internal locking, exactly 7 integer slots per entry, and every save rewrites an entity bucket's whole JSON file** | accepted as a constraint | Same brief. Safety is entirely borrowed from the same suspend convention as everything else, so it is safe under the single-writer design and would need thought under any other. The 7-slot integer limit is a compile-time constant, and the whole-file rewrite per save is a performance shape rather than a bug. Recorded because the landmark system already depends on `dfhack.persistent` and the architecture was one step away from treating it as general-purpose state storage, which these three facts say it is not. |
| 2026-09-12 | **`memory/dfhack-environment.md`'s available-tools list gains `overlay`, `workorder` and `setfps`** | accepted | All three confirmed present by direct source read at the matching version tag while verifying the architecture's dependencies, all three load-bearing (the overlay framework is the in-game-banner option for the public stream and is render-loop-gated so it needs no human input; `workorder` is currently the only route to manager work orders; `setfps` is the throttle tier's mechanism). None was named in that file before, despite the file existing specifically so that tool availability is checked rather than assumed. Flagged by the brief itself rather than noticed here. |
| 2026-09-12 | **v1 roster is three roles: Overseer, Architect, Consultant.** Quartermaster, Marshal and Chronicler are defined but not enabled | accepted, user's explicit call | Chosen over two alternatives put to the user. The narrower option (enable only the Overseer, keep the full directory structure) was this session's recommendation on the grounds that the doctrine-budget argument for splitting roles does not bite until the tool surface is large enough to approach the compliance cliff, and that a measured ~15x multi-agent token multiplier is real cost for speculative benefit. The user chose three, which is defensible on the opposite reading: **these are precisely the roles whose tools already exist** (Architect has live-verified build and dig tools; Consultant needs no new tools beyond the existing read surface), so all three can do real work rather than write proposals nothing can execute, and it exercises the propose-and-arbitrate loop for real rather than deferring the architecture's central mechanism to a later phase. Accepted cost: roughly 3x single-agent token spend, plus the lane-serialisation delay (~30s per target) showing up immediately rather than later, which makes the §4 scheduler load-bearing from day one rather than at some future scale. The three unenabled roles keep their charters and directories so enabling one later stays a config change, per §11. Revisit when Quartermaster or Chronicler tools exist. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Settled: pausing does not stall DFHack tool calls, so pause beats throttle for buying agent thinking time. Closes the same day's measure-before-relying row** | accepted, **verified from source and confirmed live** | `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-capability-checks.md` §9, added by an approved live check. Source: `Core::Update()` has no pause guard around either `doUpdate()` or the `CoreWakeup.wait()` suspend hand-off, and the only pause-aware code on that path gates a performance metric (`unpaused_ms`) rather than the call itself. Live: ten round trips against VM 103 with Uniboslan paused throughout, all between 0.66s and 1.28s, no stall and no outlier, tick counter correctly static at 170307 across all reads, pause state confirmed `true` before, immediately after, and again at report time, never unpaused and no struct write. **This changes the throttle tier's rationale**, and the user immediately corrected the first conclusion drawn from it; see the row below, which supersedes the wall-clock framing with a game-time-per-decision one. Caveats recorded rather than glossed: no unpaused comparison was run (so this is "paused calls are fast in absolute terms", which is what the design needed, not a measured paused-versus-unpaused gap); the `Core.cpp` body was read from current `master` as corroboration rather than re-pinned to `53.16-r1.1`; and DF's own call site into `Core::Update()` was not read, only the function body. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Process: a committing subagent must run in an isolated worktree, not the orchestrator's shared working tree** | accepted, learned the hard way, **no damage** | The live-check executor was launched without worktree isolation, so it ran in this session's own working tree. It correctly refused to commit to `main` (its framework forbids that), created `research/dfhack-pause-suspend-check`, committed there, and switched back, **while this session was concurrently committing the roster scaffolding to `main` in the same tree**. Nothing broke, but the tree's branch was being switched under an active orchestrator, which is precisely the file-collision class this repo's concurrency rules exist to prevent, and the agent independently flagged it from the other side: it observed two commits appearing on `main` that were not its own and correctly said so rather than assuming. Two consequences adopted. (1) **Launch any subagent that may commit with `isolation: "worktree"`.** Read-only research agents are fine in the shared tree. (2) **A branch-committing agent's output needs collecting**: its work sat on a side branch invisible to `main`, and was recovered here by cherry-pick (`5b67107`) followed by deleting the branch. An orchestrator that forgot to look would have silently lost a verified finding. Also worth noting the agent's constraint conflict: told "do not modify any other file", it drafted the `Working.md`/`DECISIONS.md` updates this repo's own rules require and then reverted them to obey the instruction, flagging the tension instead of resolving it silently. That was the right call, and the fix is for the orchestrator to own doc-sync rather than to loosen the constraint. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The unit for choosing between pause and throttle is game time elapsed per decision, not wall-clock tool latency. Throttling does buy thinking time after all** | accepted, **user's correction to this session's own conclusion** | The user corrected this session's own just-reached conclusion that throttling merely trades game speed for agent latency: throttling delays tool calls but also gives the agent more thinking time per unit of game time, the only currency the fortress experiences. Worked out: for a cycle thinking T seconds and making k tool calls at frame cap f, game time per cycle is roughly f times T plus k ticks. Lowering f shrinks the f-times-T term linearly down to a floor of k ticks, so throttling genuinely buys thinking time at the human's wall-clock expense; pause remains the only lever reaching zero game time; and the choice should follow a cycle's shape. A fourth consequence: concurrent tool calls cost about one tick regardless of count, so batched reads are game-time-efficient too. → memory/agent-architecture-and-safety.md#2026-09-12-pause-vs-throttle-unit-game-time-per-decision-not-wall-clock-latency |
| 2026-09-12 | **Deployment topology: openclaw gets its own VM; the MCP server and Sentry stay on VM 103 with DF** | accepted, both halves confirmed by the user | The split is about trust boundaries, not latency, since the persistence finding established that locality barely matters once a connection is held. **openclaw on its own VM**, user's call, and on a better argument than this session's original suggestion of putting it on the relay: the relay is the most exposed surface in the estate (public Cloudflare tunnel, unauthenticated view-only feed) while openclaw holds provider API credentials and write authority over the fort, so co-locating them would mean a relay compromise reaching the brain's keys. Two supporting reasons: openclaw's memory footprint is unmeasured, and it will be restarted constantly while roles are iterated, which on the relay would drop the public feed each time. **The MCP server stays on VM 103**, recommended by this session while the user was explicitly uncertain, then confirmed by them, on one decisive ground: **DFHack's RPC socket is unauthenticated**, so anything that can reach it has full scripting control of the game process, and moving the server off VM 103 would mean exposing that socket across the network. Supporting: the two hops have opposite traffic profiles (MCP-to-DFHack is chatty and tick-gated, openclaw-to-MCP is one batched snapshot per cycle, so keep the chatty hop local); separation buys no availability, since a server whose DF host is down has nothing to serve; and blast radius does not improve, because an attacker who can reach DFHack's RPC already owns the game host. Transport: MCP over HTTP on the tailnet, never publicly exposed. → `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §13. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The tool allowlist is enforced by the MCP server, not by openclaw's config, and role identity must be a credential rather than a claim** | accepted | Follows from the topology row above, and it corrects an overstatement made earlier the same day. When `research/2026-09-12-openclaw-primitives.md` confirmed per-agent tool scoping exists, this session recorded that principle 8 was therefore "enforceable". True, but once the brain and the fort sit on separate hosts at different trust levels, **openclaw-side scoping is defence in depth and not the boundary**: client-side enforcement is not enforcement. So `mcp/roles.py` is the authority and the host's `tools.allow/deny` is a second layer. The second requirement is sharper and would have been a real hole: if the server enforces per-role scoping it must know who is calling, and **a self-declared role header would let the Architect assert it is the Overseer and obtain write tools**, silently voiding the entire single-writer design. Therefore one credential (token) per role, issued at the seam and mapped to a role server-side, never self-asserted. Recorded before the server exists, which is the point: retrofitting authenticated identity onto a running seam is exactly the kind of change this project already knows is painful. |
| 2026-09-12 | **A specialist role's value is context partitioning, not differentiated judgment. The "accepted without modification" trigger proposed for folding a role in is withdrawn as measuring the wrong thing** | accepted, user's correction | This session had predicted the Architect role would prove foldable if its proposals were accepted unmodified most of the time, proposing an 80% trigger for folding it in. The user corrected the premise: an advisor can hold more detail, tools and doctrine without cluttering the decider's context, a value the acceptance rate doesn't test; a specialist accepted 100% of the time can still be earning its place. This session had made and then contradicted the same argument itself earlier that day, recorded here rather than dropped quietly. Doctrine is the dominant context cost, since it loads every cycle while tool output is transient; and the Overseer's own allowlist holds build/dig tools but not the matching find tools, so it can act on a candidate but cannot discover one. → memory/agent-architecture-and-safety.md#2026-09-12-a-specialist-roles-value-is-context-partitioning-not-judgment |
| 2026-09-12 | **Both safety detectors built and landed: `df-overseer-threat.lua` and `df-overseer-breach.lua`. Neither has ever been run** | accepted as code, **unverified, not deployed** | df-overseer-threat.lua admits a unit by reachability, never by a danger flag: sharing a walkable group with a living citizen, or sitting within a bounded distance of a named landmark, since each covers the other's blind spot. isDanger/isInvader/isAgitated/isGreatDanger are read and labeled HEURISTIC, used only as a ranking tiebreak; a deliberate accepted consequence is that a harmless songbird inside the walkable network will appear, ranked low. Exclusions are isDead plus isOwnCiv or the new isFortControlled, corroborated against three shipped scripts. df-overseer-breach.lua is two-stage since the Sentry polls it every few seconds: stage 1 reads one bool per map block so cost doesn't grow with dig progress; stage 2 scans only flagged blocks; severity is a rise against a session-scoped baseline. Neither detector has been deployed or run live. → memory/agent-architecture-and-safety.md#2026-09-12-safety-detectors-built-threat-breach-neither-has-ever-run |
| 2026-09-12 | **The breach detector may be inert, and this is the first thing live verification must settle** | accepted as a known risk, **recorded prominently on purpose** | Found while building it and independently re-verified on this machine: **every observed use of `block.flags.update_liquid` in the installed build's shipped scripts SETS the flag, and nothing reads it** (`exterminate.lua`, `extinguish.lua`, `spawn-liquid.lua` all write it). Whether DF's own simulation raises it during natural, unassisted liquid movement is therefore **unverified**. The detector's cheap stage-1 trigger depends on it, so if DF does not set it, the tool silently detects nothing. Recorded loudly because **a detector that never fires is worse than no detector**: it invites exactly the false confidence that the `unit-status hostile` signal already cost this project once. A second, smaller known blind spot, by design: a breach already rising at the moment of the first ever call, or right after a DFHack restart since `_G` does not survive one, is silently baselined and reported as `severity=none` for that poll. Until both are settled live, **treat flood response as covered by nothing**, exactly as before the detector existed. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Correction: `df.global.world.flows` does not exist, and the error was published in three places before being caught** | accepted as a correction, **re-verified independently** | `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-capability-checks.md` §5 named the nearest breach-polling target as `df.global.world.flows`, "the active-liquid-flow list the `liquids` tool already reads". Wrong on both counts. Found by the build it commissioned, then re-verified here against this machine's own DFHack 53.16-r1.1 install rather than taken on the builder's word: **zero hits for `world.flows` anywhere in the installed build's shipped Lua**. The related real fields are `world.orphaned_flows` and a separate per-block `flows` vector; `flow_info`/`flow_type` is an **airborne-cloud** record (miasma, steam, mist, smoke, dragonfire, fire, web, gas, vapour, ocean wave, foam, item clouds), not a liquid-volume signal; and `plugins/liquids.cpp` never touches either, so the parenthetical about the `liquids` tool was also wrong. The real per-tile signal is `block.designation[x%16][y%16].flow_size` (0-7) and `.liquid_type`, confirmed in this install's own `spawn-liquid.lua`, `deep-embark.lua`, `extinguish.lua` and `exterminate.lua`. **Propagation is the actual lesson**: the brief hedged it correctly as "not itself confirmed as sufficient", and this session then repeated it as firm in `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §4 and in the Marshal's charter without checking. All three are corrected in place, and the research file carries a §10 correction rather than a silent body edit. The failure was downstream handling of a properly-hedged lead, not the hedge. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Threat detector LIVE-VERIFIED against both failures that motivated it. Reachability-over-flags is confirmed, not just designed** | accepted, **verified live** | The first genuinely closed loop of this session. `df-overseer-threat scan` on the live fort returned exactly two units, a fox and a weasel, **both admitted solely via `shares_walkable_group_with_citizens` with no danger flag set**. Then the discriminating half, checked independently by direct raw query rather than by trusting the scan's silence: exactly two units on the map have `isDanger` **and** `isGreatDanger` true, both `DEMON_4`, and both have `getWalkableGroup = 0` and sit 109-115 tiles from the nearest landmark. **They were correctly absent from the scan.** So the design call holds empirically: reachability gates admission, flags do not, and the tool includes the class of thing the old signal missed (harmless-flagged wildlife inside the network) while excluding the class it falsely flagged (dangerous-flagged creatures that cannot reach anyone). That is both documented failures corrected in one tool, verified rather than argued. Remaining unknowns, stated: wildlife-noise volume on a larger fort, and no real ambush, sneaking creature or tame-pet case was available to test. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Breach detector: the load-bearing question is INCONCLUSIVE, and the honest reading is "unproven", not "working" and not "broken"** | accepted as unresolved | `block.flags.update_liquid` was set on **zero of 26,784 blocks on all 11 polls**, including nine spaced through a real 85-second unpaused window. That is not a clean negative. An independent full-map tile scan, bypassing the flag entirely, confirmed the map genuinely holds water: a real murky pool at z=168, one level below the fort's own z=169, plus 14,074 water tiles in total. But it is **fully settled** (`flow_size=7`, topped out, not moving), and every shipped script that writes the flag does so immediately after actively changing a tile's liquid, consistent with the flag meaning "just changed" rather than "contains liquid". The session's own absolute limits (no breach, no dig, no magma channelling) forbade creating the one condition that would distinguish "DF never sets this" from "DF sets it only on change, and nothing changed here". **So flood response remains covered by nothing**, exactly as recorded before the detector existed. Stage-1 cost was measured and is genuinely cheap: ~0.057s CPU for the full block scan. Suggested future route that respects the limits: an opportunistic test during rain, or an animal fording water, rather than a deliberate breach. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Real bug found and fixed by live verification: `designation.liquid_type` is a Lua boolean, not the `df.tile_liquid` enum** | accepted, **fixed** | `df-overseer-breach.lua` compared `hit.liquid_type == df.tile_liquid.Magma`, which is **always false** on this build, because the field binds to a plain Lua boolean (`false` = Water, `true` = Magma). Consequence had it shipped: every finding would report "Water" even for real magma, and severity could never reach `critical`, capping a magma breach at `severe`. Fixed with a deliberate dual check (`== true` OR `== df.tile_liquid.Magma`) rather than simply flipping to the boolean form, because this install's own `modtools/spawn-liquid.lua:11` hedges the same field both ways, so the binding is not safe to assume in either direction across versions. Worth noting the build agent had listed this exact representation as its own open assumption and then coded the enum comparison anyway: **flagging an uncertainty is not the same as handling it**, and only live verification caught the gap between the two. |
| 2026-09-12 | **`kill-lua` does not rescue a DF process stuck in a Lua loop, contradicting what this repo derived from source the same day** | accepted as a correction, **found the hard way** | A subagent's exploratory full-map scan allocated a `pcall` closure per tile across ~6.86M tiles, pegging `dwarfort` at 95.6% CPU for over nine minutes and blocking every subsequent `dfhack-run` call. **`kill-lua` was attempted at 20s and again at 90s and both hung.** `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-capability-checks.md` §3 had read, correctly, that `RunCommand` is registered `SF_DONT_SUSPEND` precisely so "this remote server connection could be the last chance for recovering from stuck Lua scripts". The mechanism is really in the source and did not work in practice. Recorded because the gap between the two is the useful part: **a source-verified escape hatch is not a verified escape hatch.** Recovery was algorithmic (fix the script, wait), and no VM lifecycle operation was used or needed; reaching for one first would have been the wrong instinct. Also recorded in `docs/TRAPS.md` alongside the cause: **a per-iteration closure costs roughly two orders of magnitude at map scale** (9+ minutes versus ~25 CPU-seconds for the identical scan with the closure hoisted out). Credit where due: the subagent reported its own incident in full rather than quietly recovering and omitting it. |

Canonical id scheme: strip `df-overseer-`/`.lua`, take the command's leading verb, join with a dot, collision-checked at load time. |

So this is a deferral, not a veto, and not a question to re-ask: it is a queued action, recorded as item 1 of the START HERE brief in `Working.md`. | 2026-09-12 | **`provision_vm.py clone` would have assigned the running fort's own address to openclaw's VM. Address selection is now per guest, with a pre-clone collision check** | accepted, **fixed, bug confirmed live before the fix** | provision_vm.py clone's guest_address() read only DF_VM_IP, so cloning a VM for openclaw would have assigned it VM 103's own live-fort address, confirmed live. Correct when the pool held one guest, wrong once a second was allocated with nothing connecting the two. Fixed in three parts: guest_address(env, var) takes a key name and clone --ip-var selects it, defaulting to today's behavior; DF_GW/DF_DNS stay unparameterized since both VMs share one subnet; and a pool_address_holder() collision check now runs before the clone API call, not after, so a doomed clone never leaves a half-built VM. The guard only sees this repo's own pool, so home-lab/inventory/ips.yaml allocation remains the real prerequisite. Read errors are not swallowed. → memory/infra-incidents.md#2026-09-12-provisionvmpy-clone-ip-collision-fix |

Linked clone of template 102 into the sandbox pool: <openclaw-vm-ip>/24 static via `ipconfig0` at clone time, 2048 MB ceiling with a 1024 MB balloon floor, 4 cores, onboot enabled. **Sizing was not decided unilaterally**: the live `home-lab-03` session checked SRV-01 cluster-wide (~9.17 GB available of 16.5, load ~1.5) and signed off on 2 GB, which is visibility this repo's pool-scoped token does not have. **Verified by SSH into the guest, not by the API's `status: running`**, which is the distinction that matters: hostname and `eth0 <openclaw-vm-ip>/24` both read back correct from inside. Guest reports 1042 MB total rather than 2048, which is the balloon floor and expected. **The `guests:` entry owed to home-lab was sent the moment the VM existed**, with name, address, host, clone lineage, memory, cores and purpose, rather than carried by a handover, which is the specific failure mode that left VM 103's rebuild unrecorded upstream for a week. `OPENCLAW_VMID` recorded in the gitignored `.env`; a commented placeholder added to the tracked `infra/local.example.env`, carrying the `--ip-var` warning inline so the next person meets it at the point of use. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Correction to a verification, not to a fact: a hand-rolled `.env` parser reported drift against home-lab's registry that did not exist** | accepted as a correction, **caught before it reached the other repo** | Checking that `OPENCLAW_VM_IP` matched the address `ips.yaml` had pre-allocated, an ad-hoc parser written for the comparison reported a **first-octet mismatch** and, worse, that openclaw's address did not share the fort's /24 while the registry's did. Read literally that meant the guest would boot onto a non-existent subnet with a derived gateway that could not exist. All of it was false: the parser did not strip the single quotes that `pve.load_env` strips, so the leading `'` was being compared as part of the first octet and the trailing `'` as part of the prefix. Re-running the comparison **through the real code path** (`load_env` plus `guest_address`) showed exact agreement, correct gateway derivation and correct nameserver. Recorded for two reasons. First, the near-miss direction: this would have sent a false drift report about **another repo's declared source of truth**, and this repo's rules specifically forbid reconciling that drift silently, so a false alarm would have consumed a peer session's time chasing nothing. Second, and more generally: `CLAUDE.md`'s "verify the verification" rule is usually read as a guard against a check too weak to detect a real problem, and this is the mirror image, a check strong enough to invent one. **The rule that resolved it is worth stating plainly: verify through the code path that will actually run, not a convenient re-implementation of it.** |
| 2026-09-12 | **The MCP server build is split three ways, on where the unknowns are rather than on module boundaries** | accepted, **all three dispatched** | Two genuine unknowns gate the server and one slice has none, so the slice does not wait. Research: the DFHack RPC wire protocol at 53.16-r1.1 (`research/2026-09-12-dfhack-rpc-client.md`), and the MCP server stack (`research/2026-09-12-mcp-server-stack.md`). Build: the transport-independent half, registry-to-tool-definitions plus argv construction plus bearer-token-to-role (`handoffs/2026-09-12-mcp-tool-schema.md`). **Two questions were made explicit because a wrong answer to either is a design fork, not a detail**: whether ONE RPC connection can carry concurrent requests, since `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §6's "batch a cycle's reads into one suspend window" is unreachable from a single-connection client if not; and whether `tools/list` can return a different set per caller identity, since three roles with genuinely different allowlists must share one server, and if the SDK cannot do it the fallback is a server per role. Dispatched to Sonnet agents, per the standing instruction that Opus orchestrates and does not run the hands-on loops itself. |
| 2026-09-12 | **`handoffs/` created, with executors deliberately barred from writing `Working.md`, `decisions/DECISIONS.md` and `memory/`** | accepted, **a departure from `.claude/agents/executor.md`'s own rule 3** | The convention is this repo's template's and `executor.md` already referred to `handoffs/INDEX.md`, but nothing had ever needed it until more than one stream ran at once. The departure is the point: rule 3 tells every executor to update the register itself, which with concurrent agents means several of them editing one 500-line file, and it scatters the register's voice across agents that each saw one slice. The orchestrator session owns those three surfaces and writes the rows from the executors' reported findings; executors update only their own handoff doc and their `INDEX.md` row. Also codified there: two streams must never list the same file under "touched surfaces", and if they would, they are one stream. |
| 2026-09-12 | **A DFHack RPC connection carries one request at a time, so the MCP server needs a small connection POOL, not "one persistent connection"** | accepted as a correction to `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §14 item 5 | The persistence half of that requirement is right and unchanged: holding the connection removes the SSH-setup and `dfhack-run`-spawn terms that dominate today's measured 0.66-1.28s per call, leaving latency roughly tick-bound. The count was wrong. DFHack's per-connection server thread is a strict read-request/process/reply/read-next loop, so at most one request is in flight per socket, and **§6's "batch a cycle's reads into one suspend window" is therefore unreachable from a single connection** — it needs one connection per concurrent read, and pool size becomes the real dial on the `~1 tick instead of ~k` saving. Found by a Sonnet research brief reading `RemoteServer.cpp` at tag 53.16-r1.1; the design-forking claim was then re-checked here against the *installed* build's own protocol documentation, whose "Conversation flow" section independently shows the same strictly sequential request/text/result cycle. → `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-rpc-client.md`. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Hand-roll the DFHack RPC wire protocol in ~100-150 lines rather than take a `protobuf` dependency or the one existing Python client** | accepted, on the brief's recommendation | The messages involved are flat (`CoreRunCommandRequest{command, arguments}`, `CoreTextNotification{fragments}`) with one level of nesting, so a minimal varint plus length-delimited codec covers them; `BindMethod` is not even needed, since `RunCommand` is a hardcoded id on every connection. The alternative was checked rather than assumed: every plausible PyPI name 404s, and the one client DFHack's own docs name has been unmaintained since 2021, ships no licence, needs a `protoc` build step against a sibling DFHack checkout, and **reads the 4-byte little-endian size field as a 3-byte slice**, dropping the most significant byte and so silently mis-framing any reply above 16MiB. Verified against the installed build's docs here: header is `int16 id`, 2 bytes padding, `int32 size`, little-endian; handshake is `"DFHack?\n"` plus `int32 version=1`, answered with `"DFHack!\n"`. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The MCP server can parse tool output as JSON with no sanitising** | accepted, **and it retires a trap's apparent reach** | This repo's Lua tools each `print(json.encode(...))` exactly once, which becomes exactly one `CoreTextFragment` in one text message below the protocol's 64MiB cap. Colour rides in a separate protobuf field and is never embedded in the text. So `docs/TRAPS.md`'s "`dfhack-run` colours its output even when stdout is not a tty, ending in a bare escape sequence" is a property of **that client's own terminal rendering**, not of the wire protocol, and does not follow the data to an RPC client. The trap stays as written for anyone shelling out to `dfhack-run`; it simply does not apply to the server being built. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Build the MCP server on the low-level `Server` API, not the high-level wrapper, because per-caller `tools/list` is only reachable there** | accepted, on a research brief read against the installed SDK source | The design needs one server to hand three roles genuinely different tool lists (`docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §5: the Overseer can act on a candidate and cannot discover one, and that division is held by the permission layer rather than by convention). The high-level wrapper's `list_tools()` takes no arguments and returns one static list to every caller, confirmed by direct source read; per-request identity lives only at the low-level API. **So "one server instance per role" is an unneeded fallback and is not being built.** Cost accepted: the tool definitions are assembled by hand from `mcp/registry.py`, which `mcp/tools.py` now does. Transport is streamable HTTP, identity is `TokenVerifier` mapping a bearer token to a role, and an allowlist denial returns as a tool result carrying `isError` rather than a protocol-level error, which the client raises inside its own calling code instead of showing the model. → `research/2026-09-12-mcp-server-stack.md`. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Blocking unknown before the MCP transport is built: whether openclaw can send a static bearer header at all** | open, **flagged rather than assumed** | `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §13 locks role identity as a static per-role token. The existing openclaw brief documents only OAuth and TLS/mTLS for remote MCP servers and never a plain static-bearer option, so the server half and the client half may not meet. Recorded as open rather than designed around: if openclaw genuinely cannot send a static header, either the credential scheme changes or the client config does, and both are cheaper to decide now than after the transport exists. Settles with a read of openclaw's own transport source, not another summary. |
| 2026-09-12 | **`mcp/tools.py` and `mcp/auth.py` landed, 45 tests to 86** | accepted, **merged, suite green** | The transport-independent half of the seam, deliberately dispatched before either research brief returned because it had no unknowns in it. `tools.py` turns registry plus roster into MCP tool definitions (filtered through `Roster.check`, never by re-reading a role's YAML) and turns a validated call back into exact DFHack argv; `auth.py` maps a bearer token to a role with `hmac.compare_digest` against every configured token and never logs a token value or any prefix of one. Three findings worth carrying: `TOOLS.yaml` gives `connectivity.check-units` two positional arguments both named `UNIT_ID`, and two other argument "names" are really literal choice lists (`on\|off`); argument types are a documented heuristic table confirmed against each script's own `tonumber()` call rather than guessed; and **`scripts/` has no `__init__.py`, so `pve.py`'s `load_env` is not importable from `mcp/` and `auth.py` reimplements it, leaving two copies with no import keeping them in sync.** Also found: `mcp/README.md`'s hand-written id list said 25 where the manifest has 27, missing both safety detectors -- the code derives ids and never drifted, only the prose restating them did. |
| 2026-09-12 | **A second build stream died mid-run at a session limit, and lost only its final doc pass** | accepted as process evidence, **no rework needed** | The same failure as this morning's `mcp/` registry stream, and this time the commit-as-you-go rule in `.claude/agents/executor.md` did its job: both deliverables were already committed to the worktree branch with the suite green, so recovery was reading `git log` on that branch, finishing the interrupted doc pass by hand, and merging. **The durable rule: check a terminated stream's branch before assuming anything was lost, and never re-run the stream on the assumption that it was.** The orchestrator-owns-the-register split introduced the same day helped here too, since the dead agent had written nothing into `Working.md` or this file that needed untangling. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The MCP transport's blocking unknown is closed: a static per-role bearer token works, and no OAuth machinery is needed on either side** | accepted, **source-read both sides, run against neither** | Recorded as open earlier the same day because `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §13 locks a static per-role token while the host's documented remote-MCP auth appeared to be OAuth and mTLS only. It is not: a per-server static header map is a first-class, currently-shipping config surface, and the value can come from an environment variable rather than a config file, which this public repo needs. **The one-token-per-role scheme survives intact**: the same server URL declared once per role, each entry carrying its own token, works because entries are keyed by name and not by URL. So the server is built exactly as `research/2026-09-12-mcp-server-stack.md` planned, with `TokenVerifier` mapping bearer token to role. **Honest limit: this is a read of both sides' source, never a run of one against the other.** The settling experiment is a dummy verifier plus a throwaway agent, named in the brief. → `research/2026-09-12-openclaw-mcp-auth.md`. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Correction to two earlier briefs: the per-agent tool scoping key this design recorded is harness-specific and does not apply to us** | accepted as a correction, **conclusion unchanged, mechanism different** | `docs/AGENT-ARCHITECTURE.md` §14 item 1 named a per-server agent allowlist as one of three scoping mechanisms, taken from `research/2026-09-12-openclaw-primitives.md`. That key is scoped to one particular harness by the host's own documentation and does not govern the plain agent entries this design uses; the general mechanism is the per-agent `tools.allow/deny` list, matched against MCP tool identities of the form `<server>__<tool>`. **Principle 8 still holds and per-agent scoping is still real**, so nothing in the design moves. It matters because that layer is defence in depth (§13 requirement 1): writing the wrong key into the host's config would silently remove the second layer while looking correct, and the enforced boundary would quietly be the only one left. Also flagged by the brief: the earlier research fetched the same documentation page and did not see a config key that predated it by months, so the gap was probably a fetch artefact rather than a change upstream. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Watch item, not yet a problem: this repo's MCP tool names use `__` as their separator, and the host builds its tool identities with `__` too** | open, **recorded before it bites** | `mcp/tools.py` maps canonical id `openarea.build` to MCP name `openarea__build`, and the host addresses an MCP tool as `<server>__<tool>`, so a fully-qualified identity reads `df_overseer__openarea__build` with two different meanings for the same delimiter. Nothing is broken: the host matches those identities with globs, and the repo-side reverse mapping is a dict lookup rather than string surgery, which is precisely the failure `mcp/tools.py` was written to avoid. Recorded because the first person to write a `tools.allow` pattern by hand will meet this, and because a future script short name containing `__` would make the ambiguity real rather than cosmetic. |
| 2026-09-12 | **`mcp/dfhack_client.py` landed: hand-rolled wire protocol, one persistent connection, and a pool, 86 tests to 121** | accepted, **verified only against a fake server** | Built to `research/2026-09-12-dfhack-rpc-client.md` rather than re-deriving the protocol: handshake, 8-byte framing, and a minimal varint plus length-delimited codec covering the two messages involved, with **no `protobuf` dependency**. The pool's `run_many` issues N reads concurrently so their suspend requests are pending at the same instant and DFHack services them in one window, which is the corrected form of §14 item 5's batching requirement. **The honest limit, and it is the whole risk: every test runs against a fake server written from the same spec, so a place where the VM's actual binary diverges from the pinned-tag source would pass every test here.** One real call against the live fort settles it, and the exact command is written into the handoff doc rather than left to be reconstructed. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The RPC client lives inside `mcp/`, and it is `asyncio`, not threaded** | accepted, **two small scope decisions worth naming rather than leaving to be inferred from a diff** | `mcp/README.md` used to say the package is "not an RPC client... out of scope here", written when the package was only the permission seam. That boundary moved deliberately: the client is a pure function of a socket with no server or transport in it, and splitting it into its own top-level package would buy separation from nothing. Async follows from the settled consumer: the low-level MCP `Server` API is async, so a threaded client would need bridging at every call, and the pool's batching falls out of `asyncio.gather` naturally. Neither was dictated by the stream's brief; both were chosen by reading the sibling research and are recorded so a future reader does not have to reverse-engineer the reasoning. |
| 2026-09-12 | **A dispatched worktree branch was cut from a stale ancestor of `main`, and the stream had to merge `main` in before its own prerequisites existed** | accepted as process evidence, **no work lost** | The stream's brief named files that were on `main` but not reachable from the branch it was actually given, so it fast-forwarded `main` in first. Harmless here because the branch had no unique commits, but the durable lesson is the general one: **a commit being findable in the repo does not mean it is reachable from the branch a worktree checked out.** When dispatching a stream that depends on freshly merged work, confirm the worktree branch is current with `main`, not merely that the dependency is committed somewhere. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Refinement of the scoping-key correction: the layer that would have been lost is thinner than first stated, and its importance is coupled to per-caller `tools/list` working** | accepted, **downgrades this session's own earlier framing** | The correction stands: the key two briefs recorded is harness-specific and would have applied to nothing. But "we would have silently lost defence in depth" oversells it. Because the server does **per-caller `tools/list`** (`research/2026-09-12-mcp-server-stack.md`), an advisor's agent never receives a write tool in its list at all, so a client-side allowlist is filtering an already-filtered list. Its residual value is narrow and real: two independent mechanisms must both fail, and it specifically covers a mistake in the server-side token-to-role mapping. **The part worth carrying forward is the coupling.** Per-caller listing is a source read and has never been run. If it does not hold live, the client-side allowlist stops being a second layer and becomes the only thing keeping write tools out of an advisor's prompt, at which point the right key matters enormously. **So the live test must include an advisor asking for a write tool and being refused, not only a happy-path call** -- testing only the happy path would leave both layers unexercised. Recorded because the failure mode here is a config key that parses cleanly, looks like a security control, and does nothing, which produces no signal at all. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The package is renamed `mcp/` to `dfmcp/`, because a local `mcp/` directory shadows the MCP SDK of the same name** | accepted, **forced, caught before the transport stream was dispatched** | The Python MCP SDK is imported as `mcp`. A top-level `mcp/` directory in this repo wins over site-packages for anything running with the repo root on `sys.path`, which is every `pytest` run from the root and would have been the server process itself. Confirmed by running `import mcp` from the repo root (resolved to *our* `__init__.py`) and from elsewhere (resolved to the SDK), then re-confirmed after the rename that both now reach the SDK. **So `dfmcp/server.py` could not have imported its own SDK**, and the transport stream would have hit it in its first ten minutes. Renaming was the only real option: the collision is on the top-level name, so moving the server to a different directory would not have helped, and import gymnastics would confuse every future reader of a package whose whole job is being a legible boundary. Cost was small because it was caught early: a `git mv`, six test files' absolute imports, and the live docs. **Earlier rows in this register and the dated `research/` briefs still say `mcp/` and were deliberately not rewritten**, on the same principle that keeps `working-archive/` untouched: a historical record edited to match the present is no longer a record. 121 tests pass unchanged. |
| 2026-09-12 | **The MCP server exists end to end. The project's one hard blocker is cleared** | accepted, **136 tests, nothing has met a real DFHack** | `dfmcp/server.py` ties the five existing modules into a low-level MCP `Server` over streamable HTTP: role identity read from the SDK's own auth context rather than any client-asserted header, per-caller `tools/list` from the role's own allowlist, `Roster.check` before every call, and a denial returned as a tool result carrying `isError` with the reason string intact. Built in four parallel Sonnet streams in one session, against two research briefs, on the principle that Opus orchestrates and does not run the loops. **Verified in the orchestrator session rather than accepted on report**: the load-bearing test, an advisor calling a write tool through a real SDK client, asserts `is_error`, the exact string `"Advisors do not act. Propose it."`, and that nothing reached DFHack. **The honest limit is the whole remaining risk: no live DFHack, no bound socket, no openclaw client.** Every test runs against a fake server written from the same wire spec, so a divergence between the VM's actual binary and the source the research read would pass every test here. The live smoke test is the next concrete step and its commands are written down rather than left to be reconstructed. |
| 2026-09-12 | **Correction to `research/2026-09-12-mcp-server-stack.md`, found only by running code rather than reading it** | accepted, **the brief's proposed workaround was actively wrong, not merely unverified** | The brief proposed hand-assembling the SDK's auth middleware around the whole ASGI app, to avoid an awkward required field. Building it that way breaks every server start: the SDK wraps its auth middleware around only the MCP route's endpoint, never the app's lifespan callable, and the middleware does not special-case a lifespan scope the way Starlette's own does, so a whole-app wrap intercepts startup and tries to send an HTTP-shaped 401 down a lifespan channel. Reproduced directly. **The real fix is smaller than the workaround**: the settings object needs a second field the brief did not name, and then the SDK's own lifespan-safe composition comes for free. Two related traps recorded with it: passing the token verifier **without** the auth settings produces a server that silently 401s every request, because the SDK only wires the bearer backend in when both are present; and one server app can only be lifespan-started once per instance, which shapes tests rather than deployment. **The general lesson is the one this repo keeps relearning: a source read is a hypothesis, and the first thing built on it is the experiment.** |

A bare import failure in one test file is a pytest *collection* error, which aborts the entire run: merely adding the file would have turned this repo's 121 passing tests into zero run and one error in any environment without the pin. Guarded at module scope so it skips itself instead. **So the repo's ambient baseline is deliberately "121 passed, 1 skipped", and that is correct rather than a regression**; 136 is the count with the pin installed in a dedicated venv. Recorded because the next person to see "1 skipped" will otherwise try to fix it. | 2026-09-14 | **Real bug found live: `dfmcp/server.py` cannot return a JSON array, which is what most real read tools print** | **fixed and merged 2026-09-14 (`f37502c`)**, verified locally; live re-run of checks 4 and 6 still owed. The orchestrator swapped the old `server.py` back in and confirmed both new tests (array, scalar) fail on it and pass on the fix; venv suite 138, ambient 121 + 1 skipped. The fix agent traced the gate: `mcp_types` has a version-free `CallToolResult` typed `Any` (what `server.py` builds) and per-protocol models, and `runner.py` re-validates against the negotiated version's model, where 2025-06-18 and 2025-11-25 are `dict[str, Any] \| None`. Six tools print arrays: `landmarks.list`, `openarea.find`, `diggable.find`, `chokepoints.find`, `stuckjobs.find`, `threat.scan`. | `_on_call_tool` passed `json.loads(output)` straight into `structuredContent`. The SDK only rejects a non-object there when serialising, so the client got a protocol-level `-32603 Handler returned an invalid result` (`mcp/server/runner.py`), exactly the error class the design routes around. Confirmed by re-running `df-overseer-landmarks list` on VM 103 (a bare array) and by reading the SDK: `mcp_types` says structured content is "Any JSON value on 2026-07-28; restricted to a JSON object on 2025-11-25", and the live client negotiated 2025-06-18. **Decided fix:** objects pass through unchanged; any other JSON value is wrapped as `{"result": <value>}` for every protocol version, so the shape never depends on the client; text content keeps the raw JSON. **Why 136 tests missed it:** the fake DFHack's payload was `{"candidates": [...]}`, a shape no real script prints. A fake built from the wire spec verified the wire, not the payloads. Same session: `dfmcp/requirements.txt` lacked `pyyaml` (imported by `registry.py`/`roles.py`), invisible because the ambient Python already had it. |
