| 2026-09-23 | **The `slow` tier can now clear itself, and the fort's restore target is an explicit `base_fps` rather than whatever it happened to be running at** | built and tested offline, **not deployed**; the deploy needs its own go-ahead | The gap the Chair run exposed: `slow` set FPS and wrote an advisory and nothing ever undid either except a re-arm, so every unattended run would end throttled at 10 FPS. Four choices worth recording. (1) The restore target is a new explicit `clock.arm` argument, default 100, persisted to its own state file, because reading the live FPS at the moment of the drop would silently adopt a value lowered for some unrelated reason as the fort's new normal. (2) "Cleared" means no candidate at `slow` or worse in the scan, which covers an empty candidate list and a `record_only` top entry as one case; `threats[1]` is enough because `df-overseer-threat.lua` sorts tier-dominant, verified independently rather than taken on trust. (3) Clearing runs at the same cadence as the code that sets it, so the two can never see different pictures of the fort. (4) No hysteresis, on the grounds that the pause tier has none either and the coarse scan cadence is already the only smoothing this project gives it. `clock.clear` also clears the advisory now, changing an existing verb's contract, because it is the one "I have seen this, carry on" verb and leaving it unable to clear one of the two latches would be a half fix. -> `handoffs/2026-09-23-slow-tier-clearing.md`. |
| 2026-09-23 | **The `slow`-tier clearing fix is deployed, and the fort came off 10 FPS on the live check** | done, user's go-ahead "yeah deploy" | The first deploy on this project with a live reproducible bug waiting for it rather than a change whose effect had to be inferred. Before: `fps: 10.0` on a paused fort with a `hostile_slow` advisory latched since tick 12609974, left deliberately intact at the end of the Chair run for exactly this check. After `clock clear`: `had_advisory: true`, then an independent status read showing `fps: 100.0`, no advisory, and the new `base_fps: 100`. Two files hash-matched as committed bytes at source, arrival and installed path. Re-arming was required and is easy to miss: deploying a file does not change an already-scheduled `repeat-util` callback, so the armed tripwire ran the old closure in memory until the re-arm. The MCP server restarted clean, which is real evidence the manifest and allowlists still agree, since it refuses to boot otherwise. Fort unmoved at `abs_tick 12611557`, paused, 22 alive, hunger and thirst both fine. **Still owed:** the natural-clearing check (an advisory clearing by itself as a candidate recedes) needs the fort to run and its reproducer is now consumed, so it belongs to the next unattended run. -> `evals/live/2026-09-23-clock-clearing-deploy/README.md`. |
| 2026-09-23 | **The conductor has never been able to read the game tick, and both of its time-based wake reasons have been silently dead because of it** | found live, fix dispatched offline, not yet deployed | Found while scoping the first real conductor cycle, by running `--dry-run --once` in the foreground on VM 106 against the live fort. The cycle completed cleanly and planned to wake nobody, with `status.json` recording `"game_tick": null`, while this fort has four manager orders sitting stalled, three of them `validated: true, active: false` for weeks of game time. Cause: `conductor/cycle.py`'s `_game_tick` imports `dfqueue.grade` inside a bare `except Exception`, while the same function's docstring explains that `dfqueue` is deliberately not a conductor dependency, so it imports a package it intentionally does not ship and nulls the result. Confirmed in the deployed venv: `ModuleNotFoundError: No module named 'dfqueue'`. The consequence is larger than the null: `_game_days_since` returns 0.0 on a None tick so the 7-day routine review is never due, and `evaluate_orders` returns an empty result before reading a single order, so the stalled-order poller built the day before cannot ever fire. The parser itself is correct; the fort returns exactly the string it expects. **Two lessons worth keeping:** a dry run is worth taking before a first real run, because this was invisible from the code alone and from every green test; and this is the same silent-degradation anti-pattern the creature-tag fix named, in Python rather than Lua, which suggests the pattern deserves a sweep rather than another one-off fix. -> `handoffs/2026-09-23-conductor-game-tick.md`. |
| 2026-09-23 | **The office question is answered, and the answer is that our offices were empty rectangles.** The user said it outright, and two independent live reads agree | resolved; the fix to the fort is not yet made | The Manager's own requirement, read live from this fort's entity positions: `MANAGER off=1`, with bedroom, dining and tomb all 0 and `requires_population=0`. An office value of **1**, the smallest nonzero requirement the game has, and this fort still failed it, because room value comes from what is inside the room and both Office zones are empty. Confirmed from the other direction by the room-requirements stream: the Chair that was built sits outside both zone rectangles, so neither office has ever contained a single item. Also found on the same struct, and new to this project: `required_boxes`, `required_cabinets`, `required_racks` and `required_stands`, nonzero for four positions and read by no tool here. Full position table: SHERIFF 100/100/100, CAPTAIN_OF_THE_GUARD and DUNGEON_MASTER 250/250/250, MAYOR 500/500/500, the last three also gated on a population of 50 this fort has not met. **This retires the "outdoors" explanation as the leading theory** (a current-namespace wiki page states an office need not be enclosed, and this repo's own `prefer_indoors` is labelled a preference, not a game rule); the geometry alone is sufficient. -> `research/2026-09-23-room-and-zone-requirements.md` and the `rooms` doctrine entries. |
| 2026-09-23 | **The conductor's game-tick parser is vendored, and an unreadable tick is now loud** | built and tested offline, **not deployed**; VM 106 still runs the broken code | Fixes the silent `ModuleNotFoundError` above. `conductor/game_tick.py` is stdlib-only so the service's stated dependency line (nothing but `conductor/`, `mcp` and `yaml`) is finally true, and it raises rather than returning a sentinel. The argument against, which the stream stated rather than hid: a persistent multi-cycle failure would still disable both time-based wakes with only a log line as the signal, and nobody is guaranteed to be reading journald. | 2026-09-23 | **The Architect's first real decision found the office mechanism unaided, and found a tool defect we had not noticed** | done, user's go-ahead; proposal not yet executed | `proposal-0002`, the second proposal any role has ever written for real. Told only the symptom and deliberately not told why the previous office failed, it derived that an office zone must contain the chair or its room value is 0, and stated that `zone.find` cannot produce such a site because it returns only empty rectangles. That is the exact mechanism behind this fort's two empty offices, and no human here had spotted the tool half of it. It reached this **without calling `doctrine.get`**, which it holds: the route was `zone.list-kinds`'s own metadata plus `nobles.list`/`verify`. Evidence that tool manifests teach a role more reliably than doctrine does, worth weighing before investing further in doctrine as the teaching channel. 9 calls, 0 failures, 309.6s, $0.025, on `deepseek-v4-pro` at the user's call. **It was also wrong about the fort's state**, claiming no Office zone exists where two do, because nothing in this project can enumerate existing zones and it reasoned from `landmarks.list`. Fix dispatched the same day. -> `evals/live/2026-09-23-architect-office-proposal/README.md`. |
| 2026-09-23 | **Role run timing measured for the first time, and `expected_thinking_seconds: 90` is wrong** | measured, policy not yet changed | A trivial one-turn `agent exec` with the full tool manifest costs **10 seconds** end to end including container start; tool calls themselves take 1 to 66 ms; a real research run took 309.6s for 9 calls, with one 4m21s gap between tool bursts. The conductor's clock policy computes its "closing in" rule from `expected_thinking_seconds: 90`, a figure guessed before any run on a reasoning model. Also recorded: the MCP journal sees only tool calls, so consecutive assistant turns calling nothing are invisible from the fort's side, and `conductor/runner.py` discards stderr on timeout, so a killed run cannot report where its time went. The first attempt at this run was killed at the 600s default and its zero-cost, zero-call result was mistaken for a hang; those fields are unmeasured on timeout, not zero. |
| 2026-09-24 | **The zone inventory, zone validity, furniture-aware siting and the position requirements check are deployed and live-verified** | done, user's go-ahead "sweet as go for it" | Seven files, hash-matched as committed bytes at source, arrival and installed path; MCP restarted clean, so the new grants and manifest agree. No quicksave taken and none needed: the fort was at `abs_tick 12611557`, the exact tick of the morning's confirmed `autosave 2`, and nothing in this deploy reaches game state. Three live checks. `nobles requirements MANAGER` reports `Office: required 1, status not_met, zone_ids [11]`, the first time a tool has stated the fort's actual problem, and only zone 11 appears because zone 10 is unowned. `zone list` unfiltered flags both offices in `needs_attention`, with owner state kept separate from room-value state. `zone find Office 1 1 ... AROUND_FURNITURE=true` returns the Chair at rank 2 with `furniture_building_ids: [9]`. -> `evals/live/2026-09-24-zone-nobles-deploy/README.md`. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Found on deploy: site ranking ignores whether a site contains the furniture that would give the room its value** | open, small fix, blocks a fair Architect rematch | `AROUND_FURNITURE=true` at 1x1 surfaces the Chair at rank 2, but the same search at a realistic 3x3 office footprint returns five candidates all reading `contains_qualifying_furniture: false`: the chair-containing site is either not a legal window at that size or ranks below the cut. The flag informs rather than refuses, which is correct per its brief, but informing only helps when the useful candidate is visible. For a kind with a `room_value_field`, a site containing qualifying furniture is strictly more useful than one without, so ranking should prefer it. Until it does, an advisor asking for a normal office footprint is led straight back to an empty rectangle, which is the failure this whole line of work exists to prevent. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Site ranking now prefers a site containing the furniture that would give the room its value, and `find`'s AROUND_FURNITURE response shape changed** | built and tested offline, **not deployed** | The cause was confirmed from the code, not assumed: `ranked_rects` sorts, then walks the sorted list greedily discarding any candidate that overlaps one already chosen. At a 3x3 footprint every window covering the Chair overlaps whichever empty window the distance sort placed first, so the useful site was **eliminated by the overlap filter**, not merely ranked below the cap. At 1x1 that collision is rare, which is exactly why the Chair survived to rank 2 there. Sorting furniture-containing sites first fixes it as a side effect, because one of them becomes the first chosen and nothing can overlap it away. The new key is gated on `furniture_type_ids`, which is nil on every call that does not pass AROUND_FURNITURE, so default ranking is provably unchanged and pinned by a test. **Shape change recorded deliberately:** the AROUND_FURNITURE path now returns `{results, any_contains_furniture, furniture_note}` rather than a bare array, so a caller reading rank 1 learns when no candidate qualifies instead of inferring it from five false values. Safe to change now because no live caller exists yet; the plain `find` path still returns the bare array. -> `handoffs/2026-09-24-furniture-aware-ranking.md`. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Caught before encoding: the door-for-privacy bedroom rule is folklore, not mechanics** | research accepted, rule deliberately not built | The layout prior-art pass found the current-version `Bedroom_design` page states a sleeping dwarf takes **no penalty** from others travelling through the bedroom, which undercuts the usual "every bedroom needs a door for privacy" habit. The orchestrating session had itself repeated that habit to the user an hour earlier while proposing per-kind enclosure rules, so this is the third instance of the same failure shape after `prefer_indoors` and the wiki's 20-citizen Manager claim, and the first one caught **before** it reached code. Recorded as rule 11 in the research's candidate set, phrased as an explicit non-rule ("do not reject a bedroom for lacking a door on privacy grounds") rather than as an absence, so a later stream cannot reintroduce it by assuming nobody had considered it. Doors may still earn their place for other reasons, including room value, which the sibling enclosure stream owns. -> `research/2026-09-24-room-layout-best-practices.md`. |
| 2026-09-24 | **A 13-rule candidate rule set exists for the layout checker, tagged two ways** | research accepted, nothing built from it yet | Each rule carries `invariant` (reject) or `metric` (rank), and `game mechanic` or `convention`, which is the distinction this project keeps getting wrong. Four invariants are genuine mechanics: furniture containment within a room-value zone's footprint, stair vertical alignment, a room's footprint belonging to one walkable group, and a placed room connecting to the main traffic graph. The rest are metrics, including articulation points in the traffic graph, single-corridor wings, corridor width by rooms served, workshop-to-stockpile graph distance, and a circulation-to-room tile ratio for build cost. The most speculative (a high-traffic corridor narrowing at a junction, imported wholesale from road hierarchy) is marked as such by the research itself. The companion list of rules deliberately **not** proposed, with reasons, is as useful as the table: it keeps a later stream from re-adding them by accident. -> `research/2026-09-24-room-layout-best-practices.md`. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Enclosure is not required for a zone room to count, against the user's expectation, but the evidence stops at `prior`** | research accepted; do not encode enclosure as requirement, bonus or penalty | The user asked for this specifically, believing enclosure was a game requirement. Two independent sources say otherwise (the current-namespace Office page, and a player's own direct test poking holes in a walled room and finding no value difference), and it is consistent with the install-verified fact that rooms are zones valued by their painted contents rather than by a walled-boundary scan. **Confidence is deliberately `prior`, not `verified`:** DFHack exposes no numeric room value anywhere, so no install read can confirm or deny a value effect, and the doctrine rule that only install data verifies is being honoured rather than bent because two sources agreed. The one source claiming an enclosure penalty self-caveats as migrated DF2014 content and describes the pre-v50 furniture-defined mechanic throughout; that is recorded as its own doctrine entry naming the trap that a current-namespace URL does not mean the text describes the current version. **The route to certainty is named and not taken:** build one enclosed and one open zone with matching contents and compare `getRoomDescription`. That mutates the fort and needs its own go-ahead. Also recorded: the smallest office per position remains uncomputable (no per-tile or per-item value table for 53.16), and no gate was found beyond the known population gate, with the four furniture-count fields still unresolved. -> `research/2026-09-24-room-enclosure-and-value.md`. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Rooms on this fort are built enclosed, as a design preference rather than a validity rule** | accepted, user's call ("i think id want it enclosed") | Taken after the research found enclosure is **not** a game requirement (`prior` confidence, two corroborating sources, no install read possible). The user's call stands on its own terms and lands on the correct side of this project's recurring distinction: enclosure is a **metric** that ranks a layout, never an **invariant** that fails one, so an open room is ranked lower and never reported `not_met`. Two reasons it earns its place independent of room value, neither of which the research was asked to weigh. **Theft:** this fort has a live wildlife problem, a kea that tripped the corrected `slow` tier on `theft_tag_close_range` and stayed in range for three windows, and an enclosed room with a door is a real defence against item theft. **Control:** walls and doors are also how fluids, temperature and pathing are managed, which the layout research lists as genuine door mechanics distinct from the folklore. Consequence for the build: the Manager's office is dug as a proper enclosed room rather than re-zoned around the existing Chair where it stands, which is the more expensive of the two options the user was offered and the one they chose. **The enclosure value experiment becomes optional rather than blocking**, since we are enclosing regardless; it would still convert the one genuinely uncertain claim from `prior` to `verified` if run alongside a future open room. |
| 2026-09-24 | **The room build pipeline has a fixed order, and painting the zone last would have prevented this fort's empty offices** | accepted as design input, user's own play sequence | Five stages: mine out the floor plan, mine the ore and gems it uncovers, smooth stone or replace soil with constructed wall, place furniture, then paint the zone. Two consequences worth recording. **The ordering is an invariant we violated without knowing one existed:** both Office zones were painted before any furniture was built, which is the direct cause of `getRoomDescription` reading empty for two days and of the Manager's office reading `not_met`; painted after the furniture, the read-back would have been meaningful immediately. **And it dissolves the material-knowledge constraint** rather than solving it, since stage 3 handles either outcome and no tile material needs to be known before digging, which the act/sense rule forbids anyway. Each stage boundary is a checkable postcondition, the same shape as the parked proposals-and-checks question reached from another direction. **Named gap, checked the same day: four of the five stages have tools and stage 3 does not.** Digging and vein mining are `diggable.dig`'s family, constructed walls look reachable through the generic `building` tool's existing `df.building_type.Construction` mapping, furniture is proven, zones are `zone.place`. Smoothing and engraving have **no tool at all**, zero matches in the manifest, and they are the lever that sets a room's value ceiling: MANAGER's 1 may be reachable with furniture alone, MAYOR's 500 probably is not. They are designations, not buildings, so they belong beside `diggable.dig`. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Every room is smoothed as a minimum standard, which makes the missing smoothing tool a pipeline blocker rather than a noble-only concern** | accepted, user's call ("i prefer to at least smooth all rooms") | Raised while mapping the five-stage room pipeline. Smoothing had been scoped as the lever needed to reach high room values (MAYOR 500 against MANAGER 1); the user's standard makes it mandatory on the normal path for every room, so **stage 3 is now on the critical path and has no tool at all** (zero matches for smooth or engrave in `scripts/dfhack/TOOLS.yaml`). In practice it is one stage with two branches decided by what the dig revealed: smooth where the wall is stone, construct a wall where it is soil or a mined-out vein, which needs no advance knowledge of material and so respects the act/sense rule. Engraving stays optional and stacks on top for value. Smoothing and engraving are designations rather than buildings, so they belong beside `diggable.dig`, not in the `building` tool. First cost of its kind for this project: a per-tile labour job, so a small room's walls are a real queue of fort time rather than a single job, and it will be the first thing built here that takes meaningful game time to finish. **Still to verify before encoding:** that soil genuinely cannot be smoothed in 53.16, which the two-branch design rests on. |
| 2026-09-24 | **Correction: neither branch of stage 3 has verified tool support, and the standard covers floors as well as walls** | corrects the same day's earlier entry; nothing built | Two changes to what was recorded an hour earlier. **First, a claim retracted.** That entry said constructed walls "look reachable through the generic `building` tool, which already maps `df.building_type.Construction` to `df.construction_type`". Checked properly: that mapping is the file's **only** occurrence of Construction and it sits in `SUBTYPE_ENUMS`, a table for naming a building type's numeric subtype in output, not a placement path. DF constructions are not placed the way a workshop is, so **the wall-building branch is unverified too**, and the stage-3 gap is both branches rather than smoothing alone. Recorded because the original claim was inferred from a type mapping rather than from a code path, which is the same error shape as reading `nobles.verify` as checking requirements. **Second, the standard is wider than walls.** The user also smooths floors, so a finished room is a finished *surface*, boundary and interior both: a 3x3 room is nine floor tiles plus its wall ring, several times the labour that wall-only smoothing implies. The checker rule is therefore "every wall and floor tile of a room is finished, by smoothing where natural stone or by construction where not", one invariant satisfied two ways, evaluable without knowing which branch produced it. |
| 2026-09-24 | **A constitution for district representatives: subsidiarity, with dig autonomy inside a district's own volume** | agreed in design, nothing built | The governing principle is subsidiarity, decisions made at the lowest competent level, which maps onto this project's existing irreversibility rule: reversible and local is decided in the district, irreversible or boundary-crossing escalates to the Overseer, which is already sole writer and arbitrator by design. **The user's ruling: a representative may dig within the bounds of its own designated volume.** The insight that makes this safe is that the reservation refusal in `diggable.dig` is not merely a safety net but the mechanism that **permits** autonomy: if a dig provably cannot cross into another district, digging inside your own needs no approval, whereas without enforcement every dig would have to be centralised. **Three carve-outs, all because they cross boundaries even when the dig does not:** the shared boundary wall, which is one tile both neighbours rely on and so belongs to neither side unilaterally; breaches with fort-wide consequences (aquifer, cavern, magma), which are inside your bounds right until they are everyone's problem; and digging a new level below, which is expansion rather than local work. **Two cadences, which is what prevents endless deliberation:** the council meets annually and revises the plan (a DF year is 403,200 ticks, about 67 minutes at the fort's 100 FPS cap, so six representatives at the measured five minutes a run is roughly half an hour of agent time per fort year, affordable precisely because it is annual), while contention amends the plan in between through lazy advocacy. **Interest and personality are kept separate:** interest is structural and argues for the district's needs, personality shapes voice only, because `agents/architect/model.yaml` notes a role is evaluable only while it stays read-only and idempotent, and a personality that changes decisions makes a good decision indistinguishable from a lucky one. Personality is preferred **emergent** rather than assigned, earned from the record (a district that keeps losing arguments becomes more strident), which needs no trait table and makes a better story. |
